Preface
Refined liquid fuels provide convenience for road, rail, air, and marine transportation due to their
high energy density, ease of transport and storage, and existing production and distribution
infrastructure. Biomass conversion technologies can help diversify and increase the supply of
liquid fuels toward a more robust energy future. The backbone of such development must
include reliable, high-volume biomass supply chains and efficient utilization of those resources.
These activities, if pursued, will lead to rural infrastructure development and new jobs.

In this study we focus on woody biomass conversion via catalytic fast pyrolysis (CFP) to
produce CFP oil, a partly stabilized oxygenated liquid. The CFP oil fuel intermediate can then be
hydroprocessed to produce liquid hydrocarbon fuels compatible with existing petroleum fuel
infrastructure. CFP involves fast pyrolysis with the rapid heating of biomass to ~500°C to
produce vapors that are catalytically upgraded to increase the stability of the CFP oil produced
after vapor condensation. The projections in this report are based on bench-scale experimental
results used in conceptual process models, assuming the successful scale-up of bench-scale
performance. A well-developed biomass supply chain and mature CFP technology are assumed
for future projections; results are influenced by the underlying assumptions presented.
Uncertainties associated with pioneer plants are not addressed in this report. Thus, the results
represent the future potential of this technology pathway, and not its current status. The
production and transport of biomass, along with preprocessing for compatibility with the CFP
process and conversion to CFP oil, are the most expensive steps in this pathway; hence, related
R&D can open the biggest opportunities for cost reduction.
The focus of this report is on understanding process implications and associated costs of
introducing CFP oils into hydroprocessing units. Hydroprocessing is a family of well-established
petroleum refinery operations involving reactions of petroleum feedstocks with hydrogen. These
operations help refiners achieve fuel quality and quantity goals based on market demand and
associated fuel regulations. Leveraging the existing extensive petroleum infrastructure to process
CFP oils can be economically advantageous. However, the differences in properties of
incumbent petroleum feedstocks with CFP oils, including a significant proportion of constituent
oxygen in CFP oils, require careful R&D to understand the changes necessary when CFP oils are
introduced in hydroprocessing operations designed to handle petroleum feedstocks. CFP oil
hydroprocessing, both with 100% CFP oil and coprocessing with petroleum feedstocks, is a topic
of ongoing research. Additional public domain experimental data are necessary to develop a
more comprehensive understanding of reaction behavior in various setups; this can help develop
predictive reactor models that can accelerate process development, scale-up, and refinery
adoption of CFP oils. This report does not address the required experimental efforts, but explores
other process requirements, such as changes necessary to control the reactor temperature because
of the different exothermic behavior of CFP oils compared to petroleum feedstocks.

In summary, commercial advancement of this biomass conversion pathway requires (1) the
development of an efficient, high-volume supply chain that addresses biomass feedstock quality
requirements for CFP; (2) large-volume, reliable, and efficient production of CFP oils from
biomass; and (3) further experimentation to understand CFP oil reaction behavior in
hydroprocessing units to facilitate adoption by refiners after making necessary changes to
address risks.

Executive Summary
Refined liquid fuels provide convenience for road, rail, air, and marine transportation due to their
high energy density, ease of transport and storage, and existing production and distribution
infrastructure. Distillate-range hydrocarbon fuels are expected to continue being the primary fuel
for most transportation modes in the United States. In the case of aviation, liquid jet fuels will
remain indispensable for the foreseeable future. Diversification of refinery feedstocks and co-
utilization of domestic biomass feedstocks for liquid fuels can be important aspects of a robust
energy future.

In this report we explore catalytic fast pyrolysis (CFP) oil as a potential refinery feedstock that
can be utilized to supplement petroleum sources toward the supply of infrastructure-compatible
liquid fuels. CFP oils require deoxygenation to achieve hydrocarbon fuel standards and
specifications, as well as for compatibility with any additional downstream petroleum refinery
processing. The substantial capital and infrastructure in petroleum refineries and the associated
fuel distribution network can play important roles in the practical adoption of this new feedstock
source. We present cases with conceptual model results based on bench-scale experimental
performance observed to date in the production and hydroprocessing of CFP oils. The overall
scope of this study is shown in Figure ES-1, starting from the biomass source and ending with
the production of liquid transportation fuels.

Discussion on the production of CFP oils is presented in Section 2; previous work on this topic is
leveraged to present two cases that produce CFP oils with different oxygen contents (CFP-A
with 21.6 wt % oxygen and CFP-B with 16.0 wt % oxygen). Both cases are based on bench-scale
experiments at the National Renewable Energy Laboratory (NREL). CFP-B provides better
yields and economics compared to the more near-term CFP-A technology. CFP-B is thus
considered a future case and CFP-A a near-term case. The conceptual design results presented
reflect the potential for this approach in a future scenario with a mature and efficient biomass
supply chain in the United States, along with the successful and reliable scale-up of CFP
technologies.

 Figure ES-1. Scope of this field-to-fuel study: biomass production and transport, CFP oil
production from biomass and transportation to a hydroprocessing facility, and the production of
 infrastructure-compatible liquid fuels.
Section 2 is followed by a study of CFP oil hydroprocessing cases in Section 3, which is the
primary focus of this report. Process models serve as the basis for all analysis of CFP oil
hydroprocessing. The models are exercised to highlight anticipated design and operational needs
for (1) new units constructed for stand-alone hydroprocessing and (2) understanding the impacts

 vii

on existing refinery hydroprocessing units if they are adapted to include CFP oils as a new
feedstock. Potential design and operational impacts are explored for different feed combinations
using a few simplified hydroprocessing configurations. Modeled greenhouse gas (GHG)
emissions are also quantified as part of the analysis. This report does not focus on experimental
research on catalyst development and other R&D work necessary for long-term operability of the
hydroprocessing units with CFP oil feed. The process model assumptions are based on
extrapolating short-duration hydroprocessing experiments mostly conducted using commercial
petroleum refining hydroprocessing catalysts. These experiments have shown promise of
feasibility with respect to achieving fuel quality compatible with petroleum-derived products.

Some of the biggest biomass feedstock challenges are the aggregation and processing of large
quantities of solid biomass situated at dispersed locations. This report assumes separate facilities
for the production of CFP oil, located closer to biomass resources, and potentially large,
centralized hydroprocessing facilities capable of handling CFP oil aggregated from multiple CFP
oil production facilities. Note that this analysis does not consider any policy incentives because
those incentives can vary with time and location. It also does not consider the limitations of a
less developed biomass supply chain or the technical challenges that need to be overcome in
pioneer plants; these aspects will negatively impact the economics of initial implementations and
need to be addressed via both R&D and industrial scale-up. Most financial assumptions are
consistent with previous reports sponsored by the Bioenergy Technologies Office; details are
provided in the main report along with any changes made to previous assumptions. Minimum
selling prices are reported for the CFP oil “fuel intermediate” (minimum fuel intermediate selling
price [MFISP]) and the final “fuel” (minimum fuel selling price [MFSP]) after CFP oil
hydroprocessing. A discounted cash flow rate of return (DCFROR) method is the basis for price
estimates after accounting for inputs, outputs, capital costs, etc. based on process model flows.
The minimum selling prices and other costs are reported in 2020 U.S. dollars. Yields are reported
on energy and volume bases in this report; alternate energy units are included in the summary
tables. The common use of gallons gasoline equivalent (GGE) for energy quantification does not
imply that gasoline is the primary fuel product; it is presented because it is a relatable metric in
the United States.

Production of CFP Oils and Associated Modeling
In fast pyrolysis (FP), biomass is rapidly heated to ~500°C to break down its constituent
polymeric structures into smaller molecules. These molecules include permanent gases,
condensable vapors, and solid char. The vapors yield a liquid product upon cooling, and this
liquid is of primary interest for conversion to liquid fuels. However, the liquid is unstable
because it retains significant proportions of oxygen from its parent biomass feedstock (~40 wt %
oxygen in woody biomass) in the form of reactive functional groups. CFP includes a catalyst
within the FP reactor (in situ CFP) or in a separate reactor (ex situ CFP) following the FP reactor;
CFP helps upgrade the vapors by reacting and reducing the oxygen content in a catalytic
environment. The ex situ configuration provides advantages allowing tailored reaction conditions
independent of the FP reactor and processes only the vapor/gas phase after almost all solids in
the FP reactor are removed via cyclones and other equipment; removal of solids is advantageous
for catalyst performance and maintenance. In this report we consider two different ex situ CFP
conceptual designs for potential implementation: a near-term catalyst capability case (CFP-A)
with ZSM-5 catalyst in a fluid bed and a potential future case (CFP-B) with improved catalyst
capabilities for higher CFP oil yields, along with the production of valuable chemical coproducts

 viii

with Pt/TiO2 catalyst in a fixed bed. Assumptions in the models are based on bench-scale
experimental data. A simplified block flow diagram for ex situ CFP is shown in Figure ES-2.

 Figure ES-2. Simplified block flow diagram for ex situ CFP
CFP oils are considered fuel intermediates that can be hydroprocessed to produce hydrocarbon
fuels. Some of the assumptions, metrics, and results from the analysis for the production of CFP
oils are included in Table ES-1. The respective modeled MFISPs for CFP-A and CFP-B are
$216/barrel and $148/barrel, with 800 miles of rail transportation included for the CFP oil.

For success of this technology, (1) the development of a commoditized and efficient biomass
supply chain will be important to ensure a reliable supply of biomass feedstock with requisite
quality, and (2) CFP oil yield improvements through R&D, development of valuable coproducts,
and scale-up efforts will be necessary to increase yields, lower costs, reduce risks, and enable
maturation and scale-up. Colocation of CFP oil production facilities with other industrial units
can help increase options for sharing utilities and allow more optimal use of off-gases, thermal

 ix

energy, and other coproducts from different CFP processes. 1 This can help improve both
economics and sustainability while also leveraging existing industrial logistics to reduce costs
and infrastructure uncertainties.

Hydroprocessing CFP Oils and Associated Modeling
In this report, the term hydroprocessing represents the broad family of refining processes
involving hydrotreating and hydrocracking. Hydroprocessing has traditionally been applied in
refining operations with petroleum feedstocks to improve product quality of refinery blendstocks
by (1) removing impurities such as sulfur, nitrogen, oxygen, and metals; (2) saturating olefinic
and aromatic compounds; (3) isomerizing long carbon chains; and (4) cracking to optimize
product splits. 2 Hydroprocessing oxygenated liquid intermediates from biomass and waste
organics has been a topic of growing interest. Section 3 of this report presents modeled process
metrics for CFP oil hydroprocessing, both in a stand-alone configuration and with petroleum
feedstocks like straight-run diesel (SRD), light cycle oil (LCO), and vacuum gas oil (VGO). The
petroleum refinery-centric approach applied here is intended to complement prior biorefinery-
centric analyses 3 and highlight process metrics and potential benefits of co-hydroprocessing CFP
oil. Refinery capital investment requirements for both stand-alone and coprocessing units were
estimated with input from the engineering company KBR (through a subcontract). Some relevant
hydroprocessing experimental data are included for reference (Appendix A). However, not
enough information is available at this point to allow the development of reactor models that can
predict yields for different choices of process conditions. In the absence of predictive reactor
models, we made a set of base assumptions for reactor performance. Those base assumptions,
guided by experimental data, include complete deoxygenation and 90% carbon efficiency during
the conversion of CFP oils to hydrocarbon fuels. Potential partial deoxygenation toward the
production of marine 4 and other fuels is not discussed in this report. A hydrogen-to-carbon molar
ratio (H:C) of ~1.8 for the total fuel product after hydroprocessing (for all liquid feedstock
combinations, both petroleum and CFP oils) was assumed to allow consistent interpretation of all
the results. The assumptions of nominal reaction temperatures and pressures in the models
should not be viewed in the context of reactor performance and yields. We explore sensitivity
cases to present modeled process impacts of variations from the various base assumptions. Two
simplified reactor configurations are considered in this report. For SRD feed we consider a
hydrotreater, while we include a pre-hydrotreater followed by a hydrocracker when modeling the
heavier feedstocks, LCO and VGO.

 x

 Figure ES-3. Configuration with a pre-hydrotreater followed by a hydrocracker. Reactor
temperature control options using inter-bed hydrogen and inter-bed and front-end liquid product
  recycle are discussed in the report; not all options may be exercised within the same setup.
Reactor heat management is a key concern when CFP oils are introduced in conventional
hydroprocessing designs. This concern is due to the significantly higher exotherm when
processing CFP oils that involve two major classes of exothermic reactions: (1) hydrogenation
and (2) hydrodeoxygenation. Based on existing design options in petroleum refineries, we
explored cooling via inter-bed quench using (1) hydrogen and/or (2) liquid product recycle to the
reactor (Figure ES-3). These configurations are referred to as hydrogen temperature control (H-
TC) and liquid product recycle temperature control (L-TC). An option to recycle product liquid
to only the front end of the reactor is also explored in the main report, as depicted among the
options in Figure ES-3.

Space velocity adjustments are necessary when handling CFP oils vs. petroleum feedstocks. We
adjusted weight hourly space velocities (WHSVs) based on chemical hydrogen demand (net
stoichiometric H2 consumed by the chemical reactions; this can also be calculated by subtracting
reactor outlet H2 from inlet H2) because chemical hydrogen use can be representative of reaction
demands on the catalyst. Chemical hydrogen consumption in our 100% SRD hydrotreater model,
615 scf/barrel of fresh SRD feed, and a corresponding WHSV of 2 h−1 was used as the basis.
WHSV for other cases were prorated based on their modeled chemical hydrogen demands; thus,
lower space velocities were used for the higher chemical hydrogen demand cases to account for
more reaction time needed. This generic approach was adopted to allow consistency of WHSV
assumptions across the broad range of cases explored; the availability of additional experimental
data may help guide more specific estimates in the future.

Modeled product distillation includes three cuts: (1) gasoline, (2) jet, and (3) diesel/heavies. The
distillation model was biased toward maximizing the jet cut because of the favorable jet fuel
properties imparted by constituent cycloalkanes in woody biomass CFP-derived fuels and the
projected continued growth in jet fuel demand; modeled distributions among the cuts are
included in Appendix K. We emphasize the energy value of the total liquid product and not the
distribution among the three cuts because the overlap of boiling ranges allows changing the cuts
based on refinery objectives. The modeled cuts are based purely on boiling ranges; matching fuel

 xi

properties of the product streams in the models with the desired product cuts was not a focus in
the current work but can be revisited with the availability of more experimental data and
predictive reactor models. For simplicity, only a fuel gas coproduct is assumed from the gas-
phase outputs from hydroprocessing.

Life cycle GHG emissions are calculated by combining results for CFP oil production with
additional contributions from hydroprocessing and final fuel combustion. All these calculations
were guided by Argonne National Laboratory’s Greenhouse gases, Regulated Emissions, and
Energy use in Technologies (GREET) tool. 5 As expected, hydrogen consumption is the dominant
contributor toward GHG emissions during hydroprocessing. We assumed hydrogen production
via natural gas steam reforming (without any carbon capture) for use during hydroprocessing.
Other options for large industrial-scale hydrogen production may become available in the future
and help reduce GHG emissions.

Process parameters and some simple configuration variations for hydroprocessing were explored
to help identify impacts of alternate assumptions in the models. The discussion in Section 3.2
and results presented in Section 3.4 may help provide approximate estimates for different
potential implementation strategies:

 • Stand-alone hydroprocessing of CFP oils.
 • Repurposing refinery units for processing oxygenated feedstocks.
 • Coprocessing CFP oils with petroleum feedstocks in existing refinery units, with minimal
 necessary modifications within existing constraints.
 • Augmenting refinery units to alleviate constraints for different proportions of oxygenated
 liquid intermediates like CFP oils.
Catalyst development may improve hydroprocessing yields for specific CFP oil feedstocks in the
future, but that aspect is not explored in this report. Dedicated experimental research is necessary
for catalyst improvements; that aspect cannot be addressed via the process modeling-centric
approach in this work. We assume the use of commercially relevant catalysts currently used for
petroleum feedstocks; these catalysts have been used in many of the bench-scale CFP oil
hydroprocessing experiments so far in both stand-alone and co-hydroprocessing configurations,
with positive results reported in many short-term tests (Appendix A). Some process adjustments
that may be necessary are mentioned in this report, but the list of adaptations needs to expand
with improved understanding of this process; feed system modifications are also called out.
Estimated costs for equipment modifications, because of changes in process variables like flow
rates, are also factored into cost estimates for each case (included in Appendix K).

Hydroprocessing Results
Figure ES-4 summarizes estimated costs from CFP oil, petroleum feedstocks, hydrogen
consumption, and remaining contributions when various volumetric proportions of CFP-A and
CFP-B oils are processed with SRD in a hydrotreating configuration, and with a 50/50
volumetric mixture of LCO/VGO in a pre-hydrotreating followed by hydrocracking
configuration. The modeled results below are with H-TC.

5
 M. Wang et al. 2023. Summary of Expansions and Updates in R&D GREET 2023. greet.anl.gov/publication-greet-
2023-summary.

 xii

Figure ES-4. Estimated MFSPs with cost contributions from CFP oil feed, petroleum (Pet.) feed, H2
 input, and other costs for (a) CFP-A oil with SRD, (b) CFP-B oil with SRD, (c) CFP-A oil with
 LCO/VGO, and (d) CFP-B oil with LCO/VGO in 75k barrels per day (BPD) systems. Further details
                                         in Section 3.4.3.
Figure ES-4 shows the dominance of CFP oil and petroleum feedstock costs along with hydrogen
costs in all cases at a large scale (75k BPD). Other scales and assumptions of new vs. repurposed
units are explored in the main report, and the results show that there is a $0.01–$0.02/GGE
benefit to the MFSP from larger capacities within the 30k–75k BPD range. Further capacity
reductions to the 3k–20k BPD range result in larger increases in the MFSP. Practical
implementations for CFP oil coprocessing will likely include liquid product recycle to the reactor
to maintain operating parameters such as gas and liquid flows within the design constraints of
existing equipment or to minimize deviations from conventional design parameters. Note that
any liquid recycle will likely require a corresponding curtailment of fresh liquid feed input.
Sample results for two cases with 20% CFP oil and 80% SRD feed in an inter-bed liquid recycle
configuration are shown in Figure ES-5. The H-TC cases include 20 vol % liquid recycle with
fresh feed curtailed from 75k BPD to 60k BPD. The amount of liquid recycle for the L-TC cases

 xiii

was determined by a temperature control design specification in the model; in this case the fresh
feed was curtailed to 37k BPD for 20% CFP-A/80% SRD feed and 43k BPD for 20% CFP-
B/80% SRD feed. More liquid recycle and fresh feed curtailment were necessary for CFP-A
because of the higher oxygen content of CFP-A oil (21.6%) vs. CFP-B oil (16.0%). Removal of
more oxygen increases the heat generated during exothermic hydrodeoxygenation.

Figure ES-5. MFSPs upon changing unit costs of the three major contributors: (1) petroleum feed,
  (2) CFP oil, and (3) hydrogen by 30% (1.3×) each in both H-TC (sets of four on the left) and L-TC
  (sets of four on the right) configurations. Unit cost variation impacts for these three major cost
 categories can be estimated by simple proration. Results shown for (a) 20 vol % CFP-A oil + 80%
SRD feed and (b) 20 vol % CFP-B oil with 80% SRD feed. Total liquid feed, including liquid recycle,
 is 75k BPD. Further details are available in Section 3.4.4.15.
The dominance of the three major contributors is further illustrated in Figure ES-5; as shown,
impacts of changes in unit cost can be estimated by simple prorations within each of those three
cost categories. The choices of refinery feedstocks and products, based on current market prices,
are the biggest levers for increasing refinery margins. The required hydrogen input will largely
be determined by chemical hydrogen consumption but may be influenced to a small extent by
system design/operation choices. After those choices are made, additional improvements to the

 xiv

margins are typically dictated by the small “Other” category, which includes capital charges;
other variable operating costs such as utilities, raw materials, and waste treatment; and fixed
operating costs that include maintenance costs, salaries, benefits, and insurance. In addition to
margins, the refinery on-stream factor and capacity utilization can have major economic impacts;
whenever margins are positive, lower throughputs due to reasons such as equipment downtime or
feed curtailment result in lost opportunity for increasing net revenue.

The small margins in play under the “Other” category implies that the total cost contribution of
the other parameters explored in this report is relatively small per gallon for large-scale
operations. Refinery unit designs and their operations can influence the “Other” category to
impact the bottom line; although changes in this category may be small per gallon, the overall
impact for the refinery unit can be substantial when large volumes are processed.

More than 100 hydroprocessing cases with process/parameter variations were explored in this
report; these variations primarily impact the “Other” category and, in some cases, the hydrogen
consumption. These sensitivity cases helped quantify the impacts of (1) control strategies; (2)
CFP oil oxygen content; (3) petroleum feedstock types; (4) different proportions of CFP oil and
petroleum feed during coprocessing; (5) hydrogen purity; (6) carbon efficiencies during CFP oil
conversion; (7) space velocities and catalyst lifetime; (8) H:C in the total liquid product; (9)
reactor temperature, pressure, and allowable temperature increase in the reactor (ΔT); (10) scale;
(11) capital and key operating costs; (12) new stand-alone units; (13) repurposed units; and (14)
on-stream factor. Because the reactor model is not predictive, each of these sensitivity cases
reflects the effect of single parameter variations. These results are reported in Section 3.4.4.

Conclusion
This study provides high-level cost estimates for the production of infrastructure-compatible
liquid hydrocarbon fuels from woody biomass via CFP followed by hydroprocessing of the CFP
oil. The impacts of many design/operating parameters are assessed through process modeling.
The focus of this report is on the hydroprocessing of CFP oils. While this report provides process
insights into a broad range of hydroprocessing options, there remain many gaps that need to be
addressed via experimental research, scale-up, and situation-specific risk assessments in order to
advance CFP oil stand-alone and co-hydroprocessing technologies. R&D is necessary for
enabling higher yields and valuable coproducts from CFP, along with increased process
reliability. Reliable production of reasonably priced CFP oil is the most critical step for
commercial success because CFP oil hydroprocessing has a smaller cost contribution compared
to the production of CFP oil. Finally, the development of a robust biomass supply chain is a
bottleneck for CFP technology implementation and for increasing CFP oil supply volumes for
related hydroprocessing scale-up. Results presented in this report are for conceptual mature
plants with well-developed biomass supply chains, and do not incorporate the effects of pioneer
plant uncertainties that will increase costs in initial implementations. The benefits of successful
development of this pathway can be multifaceted, reflecting the diversity of technologies and
logistics involved. Furthering biomass resource and supply chain development can lead to rural
infrastructure development and the creation of associated jobs. In the long run, the addition of
CFP oils to the portfolio of liquid feedstocks for refineries can help augment energy options with
domestic U.S. resource utilization.

 Section 1
 Introduction

 1

1 Introduction
1.1 Motivation and Background
Pyrolysis processes for biomass conversion hold promise to produce significant volumes of
oxygenated liquid intermediate feedstocks that can be further hydroprocessed to infrastructure-
compatible liquid fuels, including gasoline, jet, diesel, and marine fuel. Fats, oils, and greases
constitute the bulk of alternate liquid feedstocks that are currently hydroprocessed to liquid
hydrocarbon fuels. It is projected in the near term that fats, oils, and greases and oilseed crops
can help meet the U.S. goal to produce 3 billion gallons of alternate jet fuel by 2030 [1].
However, the projected supply growth of these resources is limited. It is necessary to diversify
and include additional liquid feedstocks/fuel intermediates for refining to augment the
contribution of alternate sources to the future U.S. fuel pool. Biomass and organic waste-derived
pyrolysis liquid intermediates have the potential to boost that supply pool. Although pyrolysis
pathways are not currently ASTM approved for jet fuel production, the potential abundance of
cycloalkanes in the product after hydroprocessing catalytic fast pyrolysis (CFP) oils holds
promise for future inclusion in jet fuel, in addition to use as fuel for terrestrial and marine
vehicles.

From a biomass supply and availability perspective, conversion technologies will compete for
the utilization of existing biomass resources. Technologies that prove higher efficiencies with
lower production costs will be the preferred consumers of available, but limited, supply of
biomass feedstocks. The 2023 Billion-Ton Study [2] provides biomass resources available in the
near term, along with future growth potential. Because we assume the use of woody feedstocks
for near-term pyrolysis conversion, based on the 2023 Billion-Ton Study we estimate a total
near-term U.S. woody feedstock potential of approximately 175 million dry short tons (MT) per
year of woody resources from forestry/wood (144 MT), forest processing waste (1 MT), logging
residues (19 MT), other forest waste (8 MT), and small-diameter trees (3 MT). Thus, successful
pyrolytic conversion and hydroprocessing technology development can be impactful toward
meeting the U.S. 2050 liquid fuel demand of 70 billion gallons per year for not easily
electrifiable sectors, including 35 billion gallons per year of aviation fuel [3]. Hypothetically, for
assessing an optimistic potential impact of this technology, if pyrolysis-based processes to liquid
hydrocarbon fuels have an efficiency of 60 gallons per dry ton of woody biomass feedstock [4],
one can estimate nearly 10 billion gallons of liquid fuel per year from the available 175 MT of
woody biomass in the United States. There are existing uses of woody resources from forestry,
but additional woody resource availability is projected in the future, such as from woody energy
crops (34–103 MT) and small-diameter trees (projected to grow from 3 MT to 35 MT) per Table
ES-2 in the 2023 Billion-Ton Study.

In this report, we explore both stand-alone and co-hydroprocessing of stable pyrolysis liquid
intermediates to inform potential transitions in this technology as more liquid feedstock options
become available for future transportation fuels. It is noted that raw fast pyrolysis liquids,
without any stabilization, are unstable and difficult to process in conventional hydroprocessing
setups [5]. The trajectory of potential implementation and success of this technology for stable
pyrolysis liquids depend on many factors, technical and beyond. There are advantages and
disadvantages of both stand-alone and coprocessing strategies for new refinery feedstocks like
CFP oils. Stand-alone processing offers the advantage of focused R&D and optimization for

 2

specific liquid intermediates, as well as clean accounting for feedstock-specific economic credits
at locations where they are offered. On the other hand, in the near term, coprocessing with the
introduction of small proportions of CFP oil in an existing petroleum refining unit can help
understand impacts and modification needs without significant new capital expenditure or
guaranteed supplies of significant volumes of CFP oil. Stand-alone processing may be possible
without large capital expenditure if existing petroleum units are repurposed and dedicated to CFP
oil processing, but this may not be possible until the supply of CFP oil is guaranteed. Similarly,
building new and dedicated CFP oil hydroprocessing units will be contingent on CFP oil
availability, and this approach also needs economic justification for the capital expenditure.
Besides helping reduce capital expenditure, coprocessing can enable interoperability of CFP oil
with petroleum sources and offer greater flexibility to maintain long-term stability with changes
in supply dynamics. Figure 1 provides an overview of the scope of this study.

 Figure 1. Scope of the field-to-fuel analysis in this study

1.1.1 Pyrolysis Liquid Intermediates
Within the family of pyrolysis liquids, we focus on CFP oils as the representative stable
pyrolysis liquid intermediate because some hydroprocessing data are available from small-scale
experiments, allowing us to extrapolate from that information to further explore uncertainties
through sensitivity studies. Past and current scale-up of CFP oil production includes efforts by
Kior [6], Anellotech [7], and Valmet [8]. Other non-catalytic pyrolysis liquids not considered in
this report include stabilized pyrolysis liquids [9] or improved pyrolysis liquids after
fractionation [10]. Hydropyrolysis at higher pressures [11] is not included in this report.

Consistent with a potential future scenario with scaled-up, commoditized, biomass-derived liquid
intermediates production, we present costs and sustainability assessments for the entire field-to-
fuel supply chain. We assume separate facilities for (1) the production of liquid intermediates
and (2) subsequent hydroprocessing to final fuels, although those facilities may be colocated.
One of the advantages of colocation is the flexibility in sourcing and generation of utilities,
which can lead to cost and sustainability benefits [12]. We present two previously explored ex
situ CFP design bases for CFP oil production: (1) a scenario with ZSM-5 catalyst [13], and (2) a
potential future higher-carbon-efficiency scenario for biomass conversion via CFP and
hydroprocessing to liquid fuels, along with the recovery of valuable coproducts, represented in
this report by a process developed at the National Renewable Energy Laboratory (NREL) using a
Pt/TiO2 CFP catalyst [4,12]. Note that this Pt/TiO2 CFP conceptual process, based on bench-
scale experimental results, included the introduction of hydrogen during CFP, thus increasing the
carbon efficiency through light hydrotreatment of CFP vapors; this CFP scenario is used to

 3

project potential improvements in CFP in future years. Options for tighter integration of utility
sources and sinks and resulting potential benefits for this pathway were discussed in a separate
article [12].

1.1.2 Hydroprocessing Toward Fuels Production
While hydroprocessing is a major processing option for the production of hydrocarbon fuels
from oxygenated biomass and its derivatives, it is not the only potentially compatible refinery
operation for processing oxygenated liquid intermediates. Fluid catalytic cracking (FCC)
processing technology has been explored for non-catalytic pyrolysis liquids [14] and CFP oils
[15]. Hydroprocessing is explored here because it usually provides higher carbon efficiencies,
because the use of reactive hydrogen in a catalytic environment helps avoid significant carbon
loss as CO, CO2, and coke during deoxygenation. Gasoline is typically the dominant product
from FCC processing, while less cracking during hydroprocessing of the same liquid feed results
in heavier fuel products such as jet and diesel. Hydroprocessing also increases the hydrogen-to-
carbon molar ratio (H:C) in the product, which is a necessity for meeting fuel property
specifications of incumbent fuels. Based on up to 5 vol % CFP oil coprocessing with VGO in a
Davison Circulating Riser representing an FCC setup [16], roughly 65% or lower proportion of
the carbon in the CFP oil is retained in various liquid cuts. The remaining ~35% carbon is
distributed between the gas phase and produced coke; the ratio of carbon in gas phase to coke
was approximately 3.5 for the overall product from CFP oil + VGO product. The gas yields
included C3 and C4 species. The gas and coke yields may be lower in commercial FCCs with
petroleum feedstocks, ~26%, including coke, fuel gas, and liquefied petroleum gas, as reported
by Gary et al. [17]. It is noted that other refinery operations such as FCCs and cokers [17] can be
attractive options for specific liquid feedstocks and product objectives and are separate areas of
research.

1.2 Techno-Economic and Sustainability Analysis Approaches
The techno-economic analysis (TEA) approach for this work is consistent with that detailed in
prior NREL biofuels technical reports [18,19]; those reports evaluated the economic potential of
efficient stand-alone biorefineries of the future that included CFP oil production followed by
hydroprocessing within the same conversion facility that may be remote and located close to
biomass feedstock production sites. In the work presented here, the team leveraged previous
work for reporting biomass feedstock production and preprocessing followed by conversion to a
CFP oil intermediate in stand-alone, greenfield biorefineries. Unlike our previous studies for
refinery integration projections [20] that used prorated assumptions from stand-alone
hydrotreating evaluations, the work presented in this report uses a petroleum-refinery-centric
view to evaluate units that import CFP oil for stand-alone or co-hydroprocessing with base
petroleum feedstocks. Most of these options may involve the transportation of significant
volumes of CFP oil to centralized hydroprocessing locations to be better aligned with potential
future volumetric growth for this conversion technology and its biomass feedstocks supply
backbone. We include an assumption of 800 miles of rail transportation of CFP oil to represent
potential future growth where economies of larger-scale operations are leveraged by collecting
CFP oil within a large radius.

Life cycle analysis was conducted to estimate the greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions associated
with hydrocarbon fuels produced from woody biomass via CFP and hydroprocessing, using

 4

Argonne National Laboratory’s R&D Greenhouse gases, Regulated Emissions, and Energy use
in Technologies (R&D GREET) model [21]. This analysis focuses on the 100-year global
warming potential, measuring estimated emissions in carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) through
the biomass feedstock-to-hydrocarbon fuel supply chain. Emissions of CO2, CH4, and N2O were
estimated with 100-year global warming potential factors of 1, 29.8, and 273, respectively [22].
Sustainability impacts in this study include the accounting of biogenic carbon incorporated in the
liquid fuels, as well as the generation of gases for heating utilities (e.g., fuel gas and steam). The
results presented later in this report provide life cycle inventories including accounting for
biogenic gases and impact of their use for utilities within an industrial infrastructure. For
example, one cannot export steam or fuel gas at a remote location where there are no other major
industrial consumers, which may be the case for CFP oil production facilities. In that case,
electricity export may be the only option for utilizing excess energy sources like fuel gas.
However, biogenic off-gases produced during hydroprocessing at a petroleum refinery may be
used as fuel gas and for steam production, thus providing economic and sustainability benefits.
In this analysis, coproduct contributions are accounted via displacement credits, because they are
small (significantly less than 50%) in comparison to the main fuel products, as detailed in the life
cycle inventory tables provided later in this report.

1.2.1 Financial Assumptions
The modeled projections in this report are based on the mature or nth-plant technology.
Additional costs associated with pioneer plants are thus not included because the purpose of this
TEA is to understand the potential impact and relevance of the research in the context of future
mature industrial implementation. A consistent set of assumptions is used for all projections. Key
assumptions are listed in Table 1. In the current analysis, the reference cost year for time-value-
of-money and all cost indices is 2020 U.S. dollars.

1.2.2 Estimation of Capital and Operating Costs
Information for the CFP oil production processes was obtained from our previous studies
[18,19]. Details for the two cases included in this report are presented in the results sections, with
further details included in Appendix E and Appendix F.

Capital costs of hydroprocessing equipment were provided by KBR; details are included in
Appendix B. Specific evaluation is necessary for each location because bottlenecks, existing
spare capacity, and other factors, along with locational costs, will be major considerations for
choosing actual designs, changes to existing facilities, and the optimization of costs. For this
report, we utilize a consistent methodology for hydroprocessing capital costs, as detailed in
Appendix B. The cost of repurposing and retrofitting refinery hydroprocessing units for handling
CFP oils is derived simplistically from the cost of new equipment, after subtracting the sunk cost
of existing capital for the same equipment at a refinery unit. Potential specific differences,
including more expenses for upgrading very old and often fully depreciated refinery equipment
compared to new equipment, are not accounted in the analysis presented.

As detailed in Appendix B, KBR provided NREL with bare equipment costs (without
installation) for hydroprocessing equipment (Table B-1) based on some specific sample process
models from NREL that provided flow rates, duties, power consumption, etc. for various
equipment. Because NREL-modeled cases explored in this report span a wide range of
conditions, it was necessary to develop a methodology to quickly estimate costs using
information from process simulations for different cases. NREL used the cost data points
provided by KBR to estimate scaling exponents to allow cost calculations for the different
processing capacities (Table B-2). Pressure corrections were applied to these estimated bare
equipment costs based on guidance from KBR, as detailed in Table B-3 and its footnotes. KBR
further provided Lang factor (fLang, below) relations to estimate the total FCI from the bare
equipment costs for various families of process equipment. These FCI estimates were used by
NREL for capital investment estimates. Details for hydroprocessing operating costs are provided
in Section 3 and related appendices.

Economic calculations were performed in Excel. New equipment costs are scaled based on
process flows in the Aspen Plus models using a scaling exponent:

1.2.3 Minimum Selling Price for CFP Oil and Fuel Products
The total capital investment, along with plant operating costs, are integrated with a discounted
cash flow analysis. Those costs, along with the gallons gasoline equivalent (GGE) of the total
fuel intermediate or the fuel blendstock products, were used to derive the minimum fuel selling
price (MFSP) in dollars per GGE. GGE is a unit of energy and easily converted to other energy
bases like gallon jet equivalent (GJE) and gallon diesel equivalent (GDE). These energy units
use lower heating values (LHVs) and are useful for providing a market-relatable cost (per gallon)
when discussing volumes and prices of liquid fuel products. In this report we use 116,090
Btu/gal for GGE, 124,307 Btu/gal for GJE, and 128,450 Btu/gal for GDE, based on GREET
[27].

1.2.4 Process Models
Aspen Plus models for stand-alone CFP biorefineries and the hydroprocessing units provide the
process basis for assessing capital costs for all scenarios. Section 2 (CFP) and Section 3

 7

(hydroprocessing) describe the models and their assumptions. The Peng-Robinson with Boston-
Mathias modifications equation of state was used throughout most of the process simulations.
The ASME 1967 steam table correlations (STEAM-TA) were used for the steam cycle
calculations. Compound slates used in the models are listed in Appendix C (for the CFP models)
and Appendix D (for the hydroprocessing models).

 Modeling and Results

2 Biomass Feedstock to CFP Oil
This section presents our assumptions for the conversion of woody biomass feedstocks to CFP
oil. This research area was funded by the Bioenergy Technologies Office since 2014; previous
experimental work, techno-economic and sustainability analyses are leveraged in this report. For
completeness of this report, key information for woody feedstocks and the conversion process is
provided or repeated here; references are provided for the interested reader to find more details.

2.1 Woody Feedstock and CFP Plant Size
We maintained woody feedstock assumptions developed from previous CFP work, as
documented by Hartley et al. 2021 [28]. Modeled costs and sustainability metrics were
developed for woody feedstocks with a 50:50 mixture of clean pine and forest residues for a
2,000 dry metric tonnes CFP plant located near the woody feedstock source. Feed composition
and cost assumptions are shown in Table 2. All costs for collection, transport, and preprocessing
including drying and size reduction are included in the modeled estimates shown below.

2.2 CFP
CFP is a process that converts solid biomass into partially deoxygenated oils suitable for
downstream upgrading. In CFP, biomass is thermally broken down in a non-oxidizing
environment. Those vapors are further decomposed and deoxygenated by acidic, metallic, or
basic catalyst active sites through a variety of mechanisms [29,30]. While CFP has been studied
in a variety of reactor configurations and geometries, the most distinctive feature of a CFP
process is whether the catalyst is included in the pyrolysis reactor with the biomass, called in situ
CFP, or if the catalyst is contained in a separate reactor, called ex situ CFP [18,31]. In situ CFP

 10

offers the advantage of operational simplicity but can be challenging to operate due to rapid
catalyst deactivation by solid contaminants (char and mineral matter) originating from the
biomass feedstock. Instead, many processes study ex situ CFP which offers the opportunity to
remove the solid contaminants after the fast pyrolysis reactor before primarily only the vapor/gas
products stream enter a separate ex situ vapor upgrading reactor; solids removal options include
cyclones for primary removal followed by guard beds or hot gas filters to further remove the
solids that remain in the vapor stream. The ex situ configuration has added benefits of allowing
independent control of pyrolysis and catalytic vapor upgrading reaction conditions, including
temperature. Other options such as hydropyrolysis at higher pressures with the inclusion of
hydrogen are being explored toward commercialization through the IH2 process [11], but not
discussed in this report; that process produces a significantly more deoxygenated oil and related
conversion and hydroprocessing metrics will be very different compared to the 16.0% and 21.6%
CFP oil oxygen contents presented in this report.

2.2.1 Ex Situ CFP in Fluid and Fixed-Bed Configurations
This work presents data collected in NREL’s 2-inch fluidized bed reactor under two unique ex
situ reactor configurations. These form the basis for two conceptual CFP plants:

 CFP-A: A current case using ZSM-5 CFP catalyst with 21.6 wt % oxygen content in the
 CFP oil, with integrated electricity production to use excess gases and heat from the CFP
       process [13].

 CFP-B: A potential future case using a Pt/TiO2 catalyst in the presence of hydrogen, with
 improved CFP yields and 16.0 wt % oxygen content in the CFP oil and multiple utility
 integration options (including hydrogen) for excess gases and heat from CFP [12].

We extend bench-scale CFP performance for the two configurations to a scaled-up conceptual
2,000 dry metric tonnes per day woody biomass plant and quantify associated cost and
sustainability metrics for a field to CFP oil assessment in this section (Section 2); metrics for the
entire field to fuel supply chain, including hydroprocessing, is presented in the next section
(Section 3).

In both CFP cases, pine feedstocks were pyrolyzed in a 2-inch reactor with bubbling bed of sand,
and char was removed from the produced vapors in a cyclone. The vapors were then sent to two
different catalytic upgrading reactor configurations described in Figure 2. Entrained-flow
reactors are shown in Figure 2, reflecting potential commercial implementations; the bench-scale
lab equipment included simpler bubbling bed systems instead of entrained systems. In the first
configuration shown in Figure 2a, an acidic zeolite was used for pyrolysis vapor upgrading in a
continually refreshed bubbling bed under an inert atmosphere. In this operating mode,
deactivated catalyst was continuously withdrawn, and fresh catalyst was continuously added to
the bubbling bed, allowing for longer operational times. Formed catalyst spheres from Johnson
Matthey composed of 80 wt % ZSM-5 with a SiO2/Al2O3 ratio of 30, and 20 wt % Al2O3 binder,
with a mean diameter of 0.5 mm were used in the bubbling bed. Use of zeolite catalysts in a fluid
catalyst bed is similar to Anellotech’s Bio-TCat in situ CFP [7], and KiOR’s BCC in situ CFP
technologies [6], and similar catalysts have been used throughout the development of the
biomass CFP technology [32]. The other CFP configuration, shown in Figure 2b, uses a fixed
bed of Pt/TiO2 in a H2 rich atmosphere to achieve pyrolysis vapor deoxygenation. Pyrolysis

 11

vapors were first passed through a 2 µm stainless steel hot gas filter before entering a fixed bed
of 0.5 wt % Pt/TiO2. Note that large-scale long-term operations may require ceramic filter
elements instead of stainless steel to avoid iron carryover via corrosion [33]. Guard beds may
also be used instead of hot gas filters to prevent particle carryover into the fixed bed reactor. In
both cases, products were collected in a condensation system consisting of combinations of
either an ambient air or a propylene glycol cooled condenser at −2°C, an electrostatic
precipitator, three dry ice cooled condensers, and an ice cooled coalescing filter. The CFP
process produces char, catalyst coke, organic and aqueous liquids, condensable gases and
permanent gases which can be quantified to give carbon balances exceeding 95% in most cases.

 Figure 2. Configuration options for ex situ CFP. In bench-scale experiments, bubbling fluidized
 bed reactors were used without the recirculation loops for sand and catalyst [34].

2.2.2 Experimental Data and Model Assumptions for Two CFP Cases
Significant CFP data has been collected at NREL in the 2014–2024 time frame, and we chose
two specific experimental results for our process modeling to represent two sufficiently different
CFP oils where the effect of oxygen contents and yields on techno-economics and sustainability
are apparent.

Table 3 collects operating conditions, biomass information, and oil properties produced under the
two CFP configurations shown in Figure 2. A sample result from literature for fast pyrolysis
alone, where the raw pyrolysis vapors are fed directly into the condensation system, are also
included for comparison. Of particular interest is the oxygen content of the oil, which can be
used to estimate the degree of deoxygenation required in downstream upgrading to produce
hydrocarbon products. Without catalytic upgrading, fast pyrolysis oils have significantly higher
oxygen contents and are chemically less stable, leading to more difficult downstream upgrading.
Furthermore, the use of H2 over Pt/TiO2 reduces coke formation and favors pyrolysis vapor
deoxygenation relative to ZSM-5 catalysts in an inert atmosphere; thus, the biomass-to-catalyst-
mass ratio is higher with Pt/TiO2. However, these yields can be influenced by changing the
biomass-to-catalyst-mass ratio for both catalytic processes. Generally, a lower ratio, using more
catalyst per unit biomass, results in a more deoxygenated oil with a lower carbon yield to oil
[35].

 12

The presence of mineral matter in the biomass feedstock, indicated by the ash content, can lower
the yield of liquid products during fast pyrolysis and consequently ex situ CFP. Historically,
most of the NREL experiments were conducted with woody biomass containing <1 wt % ash,
which is less that the ≤1.75% limit indicated in Table 2.

 Oil viscosity at 40°C,                        57                                 285                                      29 e
 cP
 Oil carboxylic acid 91d 30 34 e
 number, mg KOH/g
Data from: a Elliott et al. [36], b Griffin et al. [37] with additional previously unpublished information, c Yung et al. [38], d
Black et al. [39]. e From similar experiments. The number of decimal places shown are as reported in literature.

 13

Oil oxygen content is important because it indicates the likelihood of an intermediate oil
polymerizing due to reactions between oxygenates [40]. These polymerization reactions can lead
to rapid viscosity increases with consequent equipment and transfer line plugging. The increased
oxygen content of non-catalytic fast pyrolysis liquids also corresponds to increased acidity,
which increases the metallurgical requirements for transporting and storing fast pyrolysis liquids,
as well as downstream processing equipment relative to CFP oils [41]; previous work has shown
that steels rich in Cr are most capable of resisting corrosion by organic acids [42]. Beyond
oxygen content, fast pyrolysis liquids also have higher water contents than CFP oils because they
generally do not separate into organic and aqueous phases, in part due to the highly oxygenated
nature of their organic molecules (hence we refer to that liquid intermediate as fast pyrolysis
liquid instead of fast pyrolysis oil). It has been shown that non-catalytic pyrolysis liquids can
contain trace inorganic elements such as Na, K, and Si from the biomass feedstock [36]. These
inorganics are often below the limit of detection in CFP oils [40], potentially as a result of
deposition onto the catalyst surface [43]. However, inorganics retained in CFP oils will differ for
different technologies and producers.

In addition to bulk properties of the produced oil, the distribution of biomass feedstock mass and
carbon between different CFP products can have a major impact on the economic performance of
the process [4]. Table 4 and Table 5 collect the mass and carbon yield of each product for the
three operating modes described above. All operating modes produce similar carbon yields to
char as the char-producing fast pyrolysis reaction step is conserved among all three
configurations. In addition to not producing an aqueous phase, fast pyrolysis produces fewer
permanent gases than CFP as these gases are typically produced by catalytic deoxygenation
reactions. In the case of ZSM-5 upgrading, permanent gases are primarily CO and CO2 produced
by decarbonylation and decarboxylation reactions that aid in vapor deoxygenation. In the case of
Pt/TiO2 in the presence of H2, a larger fraction of the biomass carbon is retained in the oil phase.
The ability of Pt to activate hydrogen inhibits the deposition of carbon onto the surface of the
Pt/TiO2 catalyst, leading to a significantly lower coke carbon yield relative to the ZSM-5
catalyzed process. As with the oil properties, the selection of operating conditions influences the
distribution of carbon throughout the product fractions.

2.3 Conceptual Models and Assumptions
Woody biomass conversion to CFP oil is discussed here. Significant previous documentation
exists for similar conceptual designs and the reader can reference those for further details
[18,19]. Area numbers are consistent with documentation in our previous reports.

Conceptual processes for the two CFP options are shown in Figure 3 (CFP-A with fluidized
ZSM-5 catalyst) and Figure 4 (CFP-B with Pt/TiO2 catalyst in a fixed bed).

 • A100: Biomass feedstock handling
 • A200: Fast pyrolysis and ex situ catalytic vapor upgrading

 • A400: Hydroprocessing CFP oil is discussed in Section 3

 • A500: Utilization of off-gases
 • A600: Steam system and electricity generation
 • A700: Cooling water and other utilities

The following sections summarize the basis and assumptions for each area. Some of the
descriptions are repeated from previous publications [4,18,19] to provide an overview to the
reader without referring to other reports.

2.3.1 Area 100: Biomass Feedstock Handling

All woody biomass feedstock-related costs were modeled by Idaho National Laboratory (INL)
and emissions by Argonne National Laboratory [28]. The specifications and cost for a 50% clean
pine + 50% logging residues blend supplied to a conceptual 2,000 dry metric tonnes per day CFP
plant are shown in Table 2. A woody feedstock with 10 wt % moisture content and a nominal
particle size of 2 mm were specified. Ash content was specified as <1.75 wt % for modeling;
actual ash contents were closer to 1 wt %, as shown in Table 3. Higher ash can be detrimental for
liquid yields for both fast pyrolysis (FP) and CFP [44,45]. Woody feedstock preprocessing for
careful control of both the proportion of ash and its constituent mineral species are necessary for
FP and CFP. The direct physical impacts of ash on CFP catalysts and their deactivation may be
mitigated in an ex situ configuration by ash removal via cyclones (and filters) after the FP step
(Figure 3 and Figure 4).

2.3.2 Area 200: Fast Pyrolysis and Ex Situ Catalytic Vapor Upgrading

2.3.2.1 Area 200 Overview

  Figure 5. Fast pyrolysis followed by fluid (CFP-A) and fixed (CFP-B) bed ex situ catalytic vapor
 upgrading reactor systems
The modeled fast pyrolysis circulating dual fluidized bed system with sand as a heat carrier
includes an entrained-flow pyrolyzer and a fluidized bed char combustor, modeled to operate at
approximately 500°C and 720°C, respectively [18]. A simplified sample model is available for
interested readers [46]. A portion of the non-condensable off-gases available after the
condensation system (Area 300) is used for fluidization of biomass and sand in the entrained-
flow pyrolyzer. Entrained solids are removed from the pyrolysis vapors via two cyclones before
the vapors are sent to the ex situ catalytic upgrading reactors. The bed material (sand) and
produced char, removed by the cyclones, are sent to a fluidized bed char combustor to generate
heat for the endothermic pyrolysis reaction; the sand temperature increases upon char

 18

combustion, and the hot sand is circulated back to the pyrolysis reactor. Note that char
sequestration and other potential uses of biochar are not explored with this design choice.

For the circulating dual fluidized bed CFP system with ZSM-5 catalyst (CFP-A), a lower
pressure of approximately 43 psia (3 bar) is specified for the FP reactor in the model; the 43 psia
(3 bar) pressure is conservative, and could be lower, if downstream equipment pressure drops
alone dictate the choice of pressure. Note the configuration of the fluidized catalytic reactor
system downstream of the FP reactor is nearly identical to the FP reactor system, with circulating
catalyst, instead of sand used in the pyrolysis reactor. Vapor upgrading reactions may be
exothermic, depending on the chemistry made possible by the catalyst. Heat is also generated via
the combustion of coke deposited on the catalyst. Heat removal via catalyst coolers is necessary
in our model, since excess heat is produced in this system.

The future case fixed-bed system with Pt/TiO2 catalyst (CFP-B) is modeled to operate at a higher
pressure of 121 psia (8.3 bar) at the FP reactor, allowing potential capital cost savings from
smaller volumetric flow rates and equipment sizes. A hot gas filter is included prior to the fixed-
bed reactors in CFP-B to capture particulates and alkali and alkaline earth metals from the FP
reactor that are not removed by the cyclones, thus preventing plugging of the downstream
catalytic upgrading fixed-bed reactor and/or deactivating the CFP catalyst; alternate equipment,
such as swing guard beds, may be considered instead of a hot gas filter. A hot gas filter or guard
bed may also be included in the CFP-A configuration (fluidized system) to reduce the carryover
of solid contaminants from the fast pyrolysis reactor and help produce a better-quality CFP oil
with fewer particulates; however, it is not required in CFP-A because a portion of the catalyst is
purged on a regular basis, thus also allowing an outlet for some of the particulates. For CFP-B,
the fluidizing gases are significantly enriched using hydrogen, since the Pt/TiO2 catalyst is
designed to provide higher yields by activating hydrogen, including for partial deoxygenation
and hydrogenation, and coke prevention. This has been demonstrated in bench-scale experiments
at near-atmospheric pressures [38], and the assumption of higher pressure in the model is
conservative with respect to yields of CFP oil, since higher pressures can further activate
hydrogen and increase yields. Performance at higher pressure was not demonstrated
experimentally due to constraints of existing equipment and related safety in our lab-scale setup.
A 0.5% Pt loading on TiO2 [38] was used in the experiments and that was also the basis for this
model. In the current model the reactor requires heat removal because of exothermic upgrading
reactions. Heat management and related design and operations are also important during catalyst
regeneration; previous work showed thermal excursions during regeneration leading to
fragmentation of the catalyst at the top of the bed [47].

The CFP-A configuration is similar to the system described in detail in our 2015 design report
[18], except for higher pressure and hydrogen enrichment included in the 2015 design report (not
included in CFP-A). Equipment costs for the current system were updated by scaling based on
the volumetric flow rates of the gas/vapor phase (included in Appendix E).

For CFP-B the design basis was maintained from Dutta et al. 2016 [19], with design parameters
for reactor #1 specified in Table 2 of the reference [19] as the basis for reactor cost assumptions.
Cost details are provided in Appendix F.

 19

in Table 8.

For both CFP-A and CFP-B the yields are informed by bench-scale experimental information
presented in Section 2.2.2. CFP-B model metrics are slightly different from the metrics shown in
Section 2.2.2, because the model is based on an average of three samples; details are available in
the “FY 2020 SOT” column of Table ES-1 of a previous report [4]. Since NREL experiments
were conducted in semi-batch systems without online catalyst regeneration, assumptions needed
to be adapted for the continuous system in the process model. Catalyst circulation in CFP-A was
dictated by reactor/regenerator temperature control in the conceptual model. Although the
catalyst flow is higher in the model compared to the bench-scale experimental data, the actual
activity of equilibrium catalyst in a scaled-up system may be lower and is an important
parameter that will ultimately affect performance.

Similarly for the fixed-bed system in CFP-B, a WHSV of 5 h−1 was assumed based on total
vapors + fluidizing gases flowing through the reactor (including extra catalyst for contingency
[19]), catalyst loading was 110,214 lb in the two online reactors and 110,214 lb in the two offline
reactors undergoing regeneration. Thus, the modeled catalyst loading was 110,214 lb for
processing 183,718 lb/h biomass (for a 2,000 dry metric tonnes/day biorefinery). The
experimental biomass to catalyst ratio was 12 (Table 3); thus, the estimated reactor cycle time is
approximately 7.2 hours. Steam generation was included for heat removal from the fixed-bed
reactor system.

2.3.3 Area 300: Pyrolysis Vapor Quench and Products Recovery

2.3.3.1 Area 300 Overview

 Figure 6. Condensation and products recovery in the CFP-A and CFP-B configurations
Figure 6 shows the condensation trains for CFP-A and CFP-B for the separation of vapors from
non-condensable gases. CFP-B has additional operations compared to CFP-A because of the
recovery of hydrogen and two light oxygenated coproducts, acetone and 2-butanone (methyl
ethyl ketone [MEK]); recovery of the coproducts improve the economics and sustainability
metrics for the process.

 22

In CFP-B, the gases are enriched with supplemental hydrogen in a deliberate attempt to create a
hydrogen-rich atmosphere to favorably impact the chemistry in the presence of the Pt/TiO2
catalyst. Water constitutes a large fraction of the condensable vapors. The choice of direct
quench systems was guided by concerns about fouling indirect heat exchange equipment during
the condensation of the heavy fraction of the organic vapors. In the model, heat is recovered via
indirect heat exchange up to the estimated dew point of the vapors, 249°C (480°F) for CFP-A
and 246°C (475°F) for CFP-B, and then followed by direct quench. The first high temperature
absorber/condenser recovers the heavy organic fraction using a quench stream of recycled liquid
consisting of the light organic fraction from the bottom of the second absorber/condenser
supplied at 33°C (91°F) and 49°C (120°F) for CFP-A and CFP-B, respectively. Approximately
45 wt % (CFP-A) and 54 wt % (CFP-B) of the organic product is condensed out and recovered at
the bottom of the absorber/condenser. The overhead products from the first absorber/condenser
exit at 174°C (346°F) and 203°C (397°F) for the CFP-A and CFP-B cases, respectively, and
enter an indirect heat exchanger train to cool down the remaining vapors. Process heat recovery,
air cooling to 60°C (140°F), water cooling to 43.3°C (110°F) and chilled water cooling to 15.6°C
(60°F) for CFP-A and 32°C (90°F) for CFP-B are used in sequence. This stream then enters a
second absorber/condenser where a light organic phase and an aqueous phase are recovered as
the bottoms product and separated by decantation. The recycled light organic product is chilled
to 10°C (50°F) for CFP-A and 32°C (90°F) for CFP-B, and then used as the quench liquid for
this second condenser. Chilled water cooling for CFP-B is deliberately at a higher temperature to
allow a larger proportion of light oxygenates to remain uncondensed for subsequent recovery
from the gas phase. The light and heavy organic fractions are mixed as the product CFP oil for
hydroprocessing (discussed in Section 3). The aqueous fraction is sent to wastewater treatment.
Filters are included for all three product streams (heavy organic, light organic, aqueous). Proper
filtration to remove particulates from the organic phases is important for reducing the reactivity
of CFP oils during storage and for the maintenance of downstream hydroprocessing catalysts.

Off-Gas Recycle in CFP-A
CFP-A does not include the recovery of light oxygenates as coproducts or the addition of
hydrogen in the CFP system. Off-gases from FP/CFP reactions, along with fluidizing gases,
recovered after condensation are split; 84% of these gases recycled to the fast pyrolysis reactor
for fluidization, while the remainder is combusted in Area 500 for heat, steam, and power
generation.

Chemical Coproducts Recovery in CFP-B
NREL experimental results for fixed-bed CFP using a Pt/TiO2 catalyst [4] showed that
condensable products retained in the gas phase after the bench-scale experimental condensation
train were dominated by three light oxygenates: acetaldehyde, acetone, and MEK. These three
compounds constituted >75 wt % of the condensables. Only acetone and MEK were targeted for
recovery and purification as saleable products because of their compatibility with current market
sizes. In 2020–2021, preliminary NREL experiments showed that an adsorption/desorption
strategy can be feasible for the capture of light oxygenates. Although no problems were
encountered in the two to three cycles of operation, we understand that additional testing is
necessary to prove long-term robustness before scale-up of the system. Near-100% recovery of
acetone and MEK was achieved during the adsorption/desorption operations using silica gel as
the adsorbent, thus proving the ability to selectively adsorb light oxygenates from the complex

 23

gas/vapor stream. Following the recovery of a mixed stream of acetone, MEK, and other
compounds via desorption, further steps were necessary to achieve chemical-grade purities of the
saleable products (acetone and MEK). An extensive phase-equilibrium-based separations model
(including several distillation columns and liquid-liquid separations) was developed in Aspen
Plus for the purification of acetone and MEK using the detailed experimental speciation of the
desorbed stream. The UNIFAC-Dortmund [48] property method was used as the basis for
predicting phase equilibrium behavior. The complexities of obtaining high-purity products in the
presence of azeotropes, associated losses and energy consumption, and the use of solvents to
favorably affect phase equilibrium were captured in the model. In general, comparison of
sampled binary systems phase behavior between UNIFAC predictions and experimentally fitted
models showed reasonable parity, thus lending credence that our modeling adequately captured
the losses, achievable purities, and costs. The modeled purities of acetone and MEK were 97.7
and 99.6 wt %, respectively, with approximately 90% and 65% recovery rates of those purified
coproducts, respectively. Additional experiments, if pursued in the future, can prove long-term
robustness and further validate property predictions; increasing the recovery of saleable
coproducts can significantly benefit the economics and lower the CFP oil price. TEA information
for this section, based on the process modeling efforts described above, are included in Table 10
(cost information is included in Appendix F).

Hydrogen Recovery and Recycle in CFP-B
Following the recovery of condensable light oxygenated coproducts, the non-condensable gases
are split into (1) a purge stream (1%) to the reformer, (2) recycle to the fast pyrolysis reactor for
fluidization (65%), and (3) the balance sent to a sour water-gas shift (WGS) reactor. The inlet
temperature of the WGS reactor is 240°C (464°F) [49]. Steam for the WGS reactor is generated
from the collected waste aqueous streams using a reboiler (discussed later under Area 800). The
product from the WGS reactor is cooled, and condensed water is sent to the wastewater section.
The gases are then compressed to approximately 190 psia and cooled to 43.3°C (110°F) before
being sent to a PSA unit with assumptions of 84% hydrogen recovery at 99% purity. The PSA
off-gases are sent to the reformer section for use either as fuel or reformer feed; this split is
determined by the amount of supplemental hydrogen that needs to be generated for maintaining
the desired hydrogen partial pressure in the fluidizing gas for CFP-B and for satisfying the fuel
heat requirement of the reformer system if that becomes the limiting constraint (this is not the
limiting constraint in the CFP-B model). The hydrogen recovered in the PSA is recycled with the
fluidizing gas feed to the FP reactor. Supplemental hydrogen generated in the off-gas reformer is
added to maintain a H2 partial pressure of approximately 94 psia (6.5 bar) in the fluidizing gas
feed into the FP reactor (Table 7).

2.3.4 Area 400: CFP Oil Hydroprocessing
Our previous publications [18,19] presented CFP oil hydroprocessing under Area 400. This topic
is the primary focus of this report. Section 3 of this report is dedicated to the discussion of the
hydroprocessing of CFP oils and petroleum feedstocks in stand-alone and co-hydroprocessing
configurations.

2.3.5 Area 500: Off-Gas Utilization

2.3.5.1 Area 500 Overview
In CFP-A, electricity is the only valuable coproduct. Thus, the primary use of off-gases from
CFP is for combustion to produce heat, steam, and electricity for use in the process. Excess
energy is converted to electricity and sold to the grid.

In the CFP-B configuration with Pt/TiO2 catalyst, hydrogen is also produced to meet CFP needs.
Since we assume a stand-alone CFP facility for this base case, no excess hydrogen is produced
because of the assumed absence of local consumers or a pipeline for H2 transportation. The
presence of local industrial consumers utilities in a colocated facility may open up additional
options for the use of off-gases as fuel gas, as well as the consumption of excess heat, steam, and
hydrogen. These options and potential benefits of colocation are discussed in detail in Dutta et al.
2023 [12].

Hydrogen Production in CFP-B
A fixed-bed steam methane reformer package was used for hydrogen production. There is
sufficient off-gas fuel available in the process to provide all the heat duty necessary to run the
reformer. The reformer was modeled as a Gibbs equilibrium reactor operating at 115 psia and
880°C (1,616°F) with a 30°C (54°F) approach to equilibrium. Steam is provided from a
wastewater recycle reboiler in Area 800. The product gases are quenched and sent to high
temperature and low temperature shift reactors in series with intermediate heat exchange for
temperature control. Following the water gas shift reactors, the gases are cooled and condensate
removed. The gases are then compressed to 278 psia before feeding to a PSA. The PSA design in
this steam reformer section assumes 80% hydrogen recovery with 99% purity (note this PSA is
different from the PSA in the CFP gas recycle loop for CFP-B). Off-gases from the PSA are used
as combustion fuel. PSA recovery can vary depending on the feed gas composition and other
parameters; in an energy and hydrogen self-contained design such as this, where excess off-gas
is combusted for steam and power generation, the PSA recovery parameter should not have a
significant impact on the process economics and sustainability.

The process impacts of using biogenic off-gases for the production of hydrogen in a steam
methane reformer configuration were assessed by Dutta 2023 [50]. As in previous reports
[18,19], some of the additional capital costs beyond a steam methane reformer configuration are
accounted here through the cost of heat integration via pinch analysis, added on top of a standard
steam methane reformer cost estimate.

 27

2.3.5.2 Area 500 Design Basis
Table 11 summarizes Area 500 hydrogen production parameters for CFP-B. Further details,
including equipment costs, are available in Appendix F.

 Table 11. Hydrogen Production Parameters for CFP-B

 Hydrogen Plant Design Parameters
 Hydrogen production rate 14.8 MMscfd
 Hydrogen outlet purity 99 mol %
 Hydrogen outlet pressure 266 psia

2.3.6 Area 600: Steam System and Power Generation

2.3.6.1 Area 600 Overview
As in previous designs [18,19], a conventional steam cycle uses process heat to produce
electricity. It would be ideal if most of the energy in the biomass feedstock could be converted to
CFP oil. However, depending on the efficiency and process design, different amounts of excess
energy are available after the CFP process. In this report, CFP-B is more efficient than CFP-A
for the production of CFP oil. However, there is a significant amount of heat available from both
the CFP-A and CFP-B processes that needs to be managed efficiently to positively impact the
economics. The big sources of heat are the char combustion during fast pyrolysis and coke
burnoff from the CFP catalysts, cooling of upgraded pyrolysis vapors (including fluidization
gases), off-gas combustion, and reformed gas quench (in CFP-B). In both the CFP-A and CFP-B
designs there is excess electricity available after all in-plant demands are met. This electricity is
sold to the grid and reduces the MFSP by $0.60/GGE and $0.20/GGE for the CFP-A and CFP-B
cases, respectively. CFP-B has a lower electricity credit compared to CFP-A because more
energy is retained in the CFP-B oil product due to higher yield and lower CFP-B oil oxygen
content, and a portion of the off-gases in CFP-B are used for coproducts recovery and hydrogen
production instead of the production of electricity.

Excess heat available for power generation was determined by conducting an overall plant heat
balance. The heat balance assumptions are checked for thermodynamic viability by drawing
heating and cooling curves for pinch analysis and ensuring there is no crossover between the
curves. Electricity is generated using two steam turbines, with intermediate reheat. Pre-heaters,
steam generators, and super-heaters are integrated within the process design to generate the
steam from boiler feed water. Process condensate is recycled to the steam cycle, de-gassed,
treated, and combined with makeup water.

The steam turbine efficiencies are assumed to be 75% and the generator mechanical efficiencies
are assumed to be 97%. The first stage turbine drops the steam pressure from 1,321 psia to 350
psia. The second stage turbine drops the pressure from 345 psia to 3.5 psia. The steam inlets to
both turbine stages are preheated to 1,000°F.

Low pressure (3.5 psia) steam exhaust from the turbine is cooled by exchanging heat with other
process streams and is condensed in an air-cooled condenser. A condensate collection tank
gathers the condensate returns from indirect heat exchange along with the steam turbine

 28

condensate. The condensate from the collection tank is treated in the hot condensate polishing
unit, mixed with treated makeup water, and sent to the condensate surge tank. From the surge
tank, the water is pumped, heated to the saturation temperature, and sent to the deaerator to
remove any dissolved gases from the water. The water from the deaerator is pumped to a
pressure of 1,351 psia and then preheated to its saturation (bubble point) temperature using a
series of exchangers. Steam is generated by heat exchange with hot streams in the process. This
saturated steam is collected in the steam drum. To prevent buildup of solids, water is periodically
discharged from the steam drum (also known as blowdown). Saturated steam from the steam
drum is superheated with another series of exchangers to 538°C (1,000°F) before it goes to the
steam turbine.

The Aspen Plus model increases or decreases the boiler feed water flow rate through the steam
cycle in order to close the heat balance. The control system in the plant will function in a similar
fashion by controlling the flow rate of boiler feed water makeup to the steam system in order to
maintain levels in the deaerator and steam drum.

All compressors, pumps, fans, etc., utilize electric motor drives and are modeled and quoted
accordingly. An allowance of ~3 MW of excess power is specified to account for miscellaneous
usage which may not be accounted for in the simulation and general electric needs (such as lights
and computers).

 29
