2.3.7 Area 700: Cooling Water and Other Utilities

2.3.7.1 Area 700 Overview
Equipment list for this area is included in Appendix E (CFP-A) and Appendix F (CFP-B). A
mechanical draft cooling tower (M-701) provides cooling water to several heat exchangers in the
plant. The tower utilizes fans to force air through circulated water.

An instrument air system is included to provide compressed air for both service and instruments.
The system consists of an air compressor (K-701), dryer (S-701), and receiver (T-701). The
instrument air is delivered at a pressure of 115 psia and a moisture dew point of −40°C (−40°F),
and it is oil free.

Other miscellaneous items that are taken into account in the design include the following:

 • Firewater storage tank (T-702) and firewater pump (P-702) for fire emergencies
 • Diesel tank (T-703) and diesel pump (P-703) to fuel the front loaders
 • Ammonia storage tank (T-704), ammonia pump (P-704), boiler feed water chemical
 storage tank (T-708), and boiler feed water chemical pump (P-707) for boiler feed water
 treatment.
 • Caustic (NaOH) storage tank (T-705) and pump (P-705).

2.3.8 Area 800: Wastewater Management and Recycle

2.3.8.1 Area 800 Overview
There are differences in the Area 800 designs for CFP-A and CFP-B because CFP-B includes
hydrogen use and production; hydrogen production requires the use of steam in the reformer and
the WGS reactor. The general design for both systems is based on the use of a regenerative
thermal oxidizer for the combustion of organics, as detailed in our previous design report [18].

For CFP-B Area 800 has three process sub-areas for treating and recycling water discharges from
the plant: a wastewater boiler, a regenerative thermal oxidizer, and a reverse osmosis unit for
point treatment. The majority of the soluble compounds containing carbon is found in the
aqueous phase of the pyrolysis quench loop in Area 300. This stream, with a significantly
smaller makeup of clean recycled water is sent to a waste heat boiler system to first generate
steam for process use in the sour WGS reactor in Area 300. Any H2S present in the wastewater is
expected to be stripped out of the water during this step. A subsequent boiler generates steam for
the reformer; small proportions of carbon species are also part of the steam and are expected to
be utilized in the pre-reformer/reformer system for hydrogen production. Generating steam from
the CFP quench water serves to recycle some of the volatile carbon species back to the process,
and also to concentrate the remaining soluble species like phenol. To avoid fouling in the boiler,
not all the wastewater is evaporated. This stream, more concentrated with carbon species, is sent
to a regenerative thermal oxidizer to combust the organics. For CFP-A the steam generation step
is skipped, and the entire CFP aqueous phase is sent to the regenerative thermal oxidizer.

An on-site reverse osmosis unit is specified for point treatment and recycle of streams with low
contamination. These include the cooling tower blowdown, water knockout from the PSA
compressor in Area 300, and the flue gas scrubber effluent from Area 100. We assume that 75%
of the water can be recovered and recycled to the flue gas scrubber, Area 600 boiler, and cooling
tower to offset the total water demand for the process. The remaining 25% is collected for
treatment, along with the hydroprocessing water knockout streams, which were considered
unsuitable for the boiler and regenerative thermal oxidizer due to nitrogen (ammonia) content.
While we did not perform a detailed design for an on-site wastewater treatment plant, it is
expected that such treatment will entail demineralization of the reverse osmosis retentate streams
and probably activated sludge denitrification of the hydroprocessing water, followed by aerobic
digestion and polishing. Anaerobic digestion is not likely to be economical due to the low
remaining chemical oxygen demand (COD). We estimated an operating cost charge for
wastewater using the aggregate CAPEX+OPEX cost contribution of the detailed wastewater
facility designed for the 2011 biochemical ethanol design report [52]. At $0.022/gal wastewater,
this conservatively estimated cost of treating the relatively less contaminated streams from the
pyrolysis plant is a factor of 10 higher than the value cited in Peters and Timmerhaus [53] for
treatment of boiler blowdown streams.

Table 15 summarizes the water balance for the plant. The consumptive water demand for the
process is 2.80 gal/GGE for CFP-A and 1.19 gal/GGE for CFP-B. Along with uncertainties
surrounding the choice of equipment in Area 800, we note that there could also be significant
future opportunities to recover valuable products from the wastewater using processes such as
aqueous phase reforming or biological upgrading if the CFP process is commercially established.

2.3.10 Pinch Analysis
While an energy balance was performed within the Aspen Plus process simulation, it was also
necessary to ensure that there was no temperature crossover when the assumption was made that
the cold process streams can be heated using the available heat in hot process streams. A pinch
analysis was performed to show the temperatures at which hot and cold duties are available. This
step is included in our thermochemical analysis to ensure thermodynamic integrity of our heat
exchange assumptions [18]. For CFP-A and CFP-B the ΔTmin were 39.2°F and 16.8°F,
respectively, with total duties of in-process exchange 969 MMBtu and 999 MMBtu, respectively.
The heat exchange network cost was estimated by using the in-process exchange duties as
scaling variables and a scaling exponent of 0.65, with base cost and duty information derived
from the ex situ network in the 2015 design report [18].

2.3.12 Process Economics
This section describes how the process model, detailed design, and cost estimation discussed so
far is used to determine the economics of CFP oil production. The analysis is based entirely on
estimated equipment costs and regular construction and project management costs associated
with building a plant of this kind along with associated operating costs. The analysis does not
consider any policy factors such as carbon credits, subsidies, or mandates.

The FCI is first computed using a comprehensive list of process and mechanical equipment in
the plant and its expected construction and project costs. Variable and fixed operating costs
associated with plant operation and maintenance are determined next. With these estimated costs,
a discounted cash flow analysis is used to determine a minimum fuel intermediate selling price
(MFISP) that resolves to a net present value of zero for the entire project at some assumed
nonzero discount rate, also referred to in this report as the internal rate of return. In other words,
given the production rates determined in the process model, the CFP oil fuel intermediate from
the plant must be sold at its MFISP in order for the project to return the specified internal rate of
return and no more. This section describes the calculations and assumptions made in completing
the discounted cash flow analysis.

2.3.12.1 Total Capital Investment
This section summarizes the purchased and installed equipment costs for CFP-A and CFP-B. For
the CFP processes in this report, the total installed costs for the plant serve as the basis for
determining the total capital investment. Most of the equipment costs are from previous work
[18,19] and were derived from various sources including equipment suppliers and licensors, cost
estimates from Harris Group, published literature, and Aspen Capital Cost Estimator software.
The reader should refer to the previous references [18,19] for further details. A complete listing

of the equipment, along with its purchased and installed costs are included in Appendix E (CFP-

2.3.12.4 Discounted Cash Flow Analysis and the MFISP
Once the capital and operating costs are determined, an MFISP is determined using a discounted
cash flow rate of return (DCFROR) analysis. The general methodology used is the same as that
applied in previous NREL reports [4,18], and example spreadsheets are available publicly [46].
We normalize the CFP oil intermediate on an LHV energy basis. In this case we use a single
gasoline-equivalent product for calculations, although any other basis could be used, and costs
can easily be converted to other energy units of interest. The discounted cash flow analysis
determines the minimum selling price of a gallon of this gasoline-equivalent product, which
yields a net present value of zero for the project. The financial parameters used to compute the
net present value are as follows.

Discount Rate/Internal Rate of Return: For this analysis, the cash flow discount rate was set to
an internal rate of return of 10%.

Equity Financing: For this analysis, it was assumed that the plant would be 40% equity financed.
The terms of the loan on the remaining 60% debt were taken to be 8% interest for 10 years. The
principal is taken out in stages over the 3-year construction period. Interest on the loan is paid
during this 3-year period, but principal is not paid back. (This implies that cash flow comes from
a parent company until the plant starts up.)

Depreciation: Capital depreciation is computed according to the IRS Modified Accelerated Cost
Recovery System. The bulk of the plant capital is depreciated over a 7-year recovery period.
Because the plant described here is a net exporter of electricity, the steam plant and power
generation equipment is depreciated over a 20-year recovery period, according to the IRS.

Taxes: The federal corporate tax rate used in our analysis is 21%. Income tax is averaged over
the plant life and that average is calculated on a per-GGE basis. The amount of income tax to be
paid varies annually with changes in the volume of product produced and the allowable
depreciation deduction. In fact, no income tax is paid in the first 8 years of operation because
depreciation and loan interest deductions are greater than the net income. State taxes are not
considered, primarily because the location of the plant has not been determined and tax rates
vary from state to state (from 0% to 12%).

Construction Time: The construction time was taken to be 24 months. Twelve months are added
before construction for planning and engineering.

Startup Time: Startup was taken to be 25% of the construction time, or 6 months in this case. It is
assumed that the plant achieves 50% production during the startup period while incurring 75% of
variable expenses and 100% of fixed expenses.

Lifetime: The plant and all equipment are assumed to have a 30-year life. No salvage value is
assumed at the end of 30 years.

Working Capital: Working capital was taken to be 5% of the FCI.

 40

B). As expected, CFP-B has a lower MFISP than CFP-A because of higher CFP oil yield and the
benefits from the co-production of saleable chemical coproducts.

2.3.13 Sustainability and Efficiency Metrics
We present the life cycle inventory along with R&D GREET 2023 [27] based emission factors
for GHG calculations. The values presented are for a 2,000 dry metric tonnes per day plant for
CFP-A (Table 26) and CFP-B (Table 27). CFP-A produces more electricity at the expense of a
lower CFP oil yield. From a high-level U.S. resource standpoint, the desirable outcome is to
produce more liquid fuels via CFP-B from scarce biomass resources instead of producing
electricity, even though current emission factors indicate that CFP-A is more sustainable from a
GHG emissions standpoint. It is expected that the emission factor for displacing the electricity
will continue to go down in the future as solar and wind deployments increase, and the GHG
emissions accounting difference between CFP-A and CFP-B may progressively narrow down as
overall carbon intensity for U.S. grid electricity declines.

 Section 3
CFP Oil Hydroprocessing: Techno-Economics
 and Supply Chain Sustainability Metrics for
 Hydrocarbon Fuel Product
 Modeling and Results

 47

3 Hydroprocessing
We use the term hydroprocessing to represent the broad family of refining processes involving
hydrotreating and hydrocracking. Hydroprocessing has traditionally been applied in refining
operations with petroleum feedstocks to improve product quality of refinery blendstocks by (1)
removing impurities such as sulfur, nitrogen, oxygen, and metals, (2) saturating olefinic and
aromatic compounds, (3) isomerizing long carbon chains, and (4) cracking to optimize product
splits [17]. Hydroprocessing oxygenated liquid intermediates from biomass and waste organics
has been a topic of recent interest for fuels and chemicals production. Publications on this topic
continue to grow with the availability of experimental results for specific feedstocks. A 2013
review article on this topic discussed a broad set of feedstocks [58]; additional references
relevant to this report are included in the following discussions. The focus of this report is on
process metrics when CFP oils are hydroprocessed either in a stand-alone configuration or
coprocessed with other feedstocks like straight-run diesel (SRD), light cycle oil (LCO), and
vacuum gas oil (VGO). Catalyst development to increase effectiveness is not a focus area of this
report; we assume the use of commercially relevant catalysts for petroleum feedstocks, since
limited experimental data so far has shown that those catalysts also work for upgrading CFP oils
[13,38,59,60]. Research on catalyst activity and maintenance is necessary for long-term success,
but we are not at a point where predictive reaction behavior can be incorporated into process
models. Design and operational impacts are assessed in this study by including a consistent set of
assumptions for reactor behavior; impacts of changing those assumptions are explored via
sensitivity analysis. This report includes a premise of co-utilization or reutilization of petroleum
technology and infrastructure for processing CFP oils; this is based on short-duration bench-scale
experiments with CFP oils [13,59,61]; further R&D is necessary to assess, validate or invalidate,
and provide solutions to enable the use of current infrastructure. Along with the differences in
reaction behavior of CFP oil constituents, hydrogen consumption and related reactor heat
management stand out as the most significant process impacts because of high hydrogen
requirement for hydroprocessing CFP oils to hydrocarbon fuels. In this context, it should be
noted that the range of oxygen contents in the CFP oils in this report (16 to 22 wt %) are
significantly higher than typical proportions of N and S heteroatoms present in petroleum
hydroprocessing feedstocks; in addition, oxygen removal from difficult-to-hydrodeoxygenate
phenolic bonds in CFP oils, usually needs to be facilitated by the saturation of aromatic rings
before the removal of that oxygen [62]. As a concurrent benefit, the saturation of aromatic rings
result in cycloalkanes that are desirable for jet fuel [63] and also improve the fuel’s cetane
number [64]. Temperature control using hydrogen and/or liquid recycle injection into the reactor
are explored. Additional anticipated process impacts are also discussed in this report. These
hydroprocessing challenges exist for other oxygenated feedstocks and the learnings from this
report may be extended to other liquid feedstocks.

Our goal is to present cases and results that can be adapted to understand key process
considerations associated with various potential implementations, which may include:
 • Stand-alone hydroprocessing of CFP oils
 • Repurposing refinery units for processing oxygenated feedstocks
 • Coprocessing CFP oils with petroleum feedstocks in existing refinery units with minimal
 necessary modifications within existing constraints

 48

 • Augmenting refinery units to alleviate constraints for processing large volumes of
 oxygenated liquid intermediates like CFP oils
Given the breadth, depth, and diversity in implementation, we attempt to illustrate these aspects
through variations within a set of a few base configurations discussed in Sections 3.2.2 and 3.2.4.
Consistent assumptions are maintained to understand potential impacts. Those assumptions may
differ for specific implementations, but the directional trends reported will likely be maintained
for a broad set of conditions and configurations. In some cases, these assumptions lead to
modeled operational parameters that are beyond the feasibility of current implementations; those
results are useful for informing the adoption of some generic alternate strategies and may help
inform the most effective choices unique to each installation. When trying to understand the
potential use of any existing hydroprocessing facility for processing or coprocessing CFP oils,
the parameter space for those studies will be more specific and evident and may include some of
the aspects discussed later in this report.

3.1 Experimental Data for CFP Oil Hydroprocessing
CFP oils have been hydrotreated in continuous trickle-bed reactors for both stand-alone
hydrotreating and co-hydroprocessing scenarios. Data for stand-alone hydrotreating is compiled
in Appendix A, Table A-1. A wide variety of CFP oils, produced over HZSM-5 [13,65], Pt/TiO2
[60,65], alumina [66,67], and red mud [68,69,70] catalysts and with oxygen contents of 16–29
wt % on a dry basis (db) have been processed. The longest published times on stream exceed 300
h, and these comprise processing CFP oils with high oxygen contents of 25-28 wt % oxygen (db)
[67,68]. NREL has since completed CFP oil hydrotreating for 588 h with CFP oil (19–23 wt % O
db) without plugging or fouling issues reported. A single-stage hydrotreating possible with CFP
oils contrasts with stand-alone hydroprocessing of non-catalytic fast pyrolysis liquids where a
separate reactor with catalysts such as Ru/C are used to first stabilize the liquid before further
hydroprocessing [9].

Sulfided NiMo has been the catalyst of choice for most of the studies though sulfided CoMo [68]
gave stable operation for over 300 h for a product with 1 wt % oxygen. Sulfur contents of CFP
oils from woody biomass are typically lower than necessary to keep the catalyst sulfided, and
H2S or sulfiding agents need to be added during stand-alone processing; coprocessing with high
sulfur petroleum feed may also provide the necessary sulfur. A co-hydrotreating study showed
better hydrogenation with NiMo than with CoMo, leading to a higher H:C ratio in the product
and a higher cetane number in the diesel-range cut [60]. Hydrogenation of aromatic rings is also
an advantage for jet fuel since cycloalkanes have more favorable properties than aromatics [65].

Stand-alone hydrotreating temperatures approaching 400°C are required for good deoxygenation;
operations at 300°C or below lead to the product oxygen content increasing significantly during
the run (e.g., from 1 to 11 wt %) [66,67], whereas the product oxygen contents remained stable at
385°C–400°C in most of the experiments. Phenols are prevalent compounds in CFP oils and
their deoxygenation is well known to require high temperatures [71]. Hydrogenation of the
aromatic rings in phenols at lower temperatures produces cyclohexanols, and those alcohols can
be hydrodeoxygenated at lower temperatures than phenols. However, at lower temperatures, the
hydrogenation activity of the catalysts decreases with time on stream and therefore, the fraction
of phenols in the product increases, leading to the increase in the product oxygen content
measured over time at low temperatures.

 49

Experiments at 385°C–400°C have yielded product oxygen contents of <0.01 to approximately 1
wt %, with a tendency of CFP oils with oxygen contents above 20 wt % yielding products with
~1 wt % oxygen and those with <20 wt % O (db) giving lower values at similar conditions
(Figure 7a). The reported carbon efficiencies to liquid hydrocarbons ranged from 83 to 93%, and
in general decreased as the CFP oil oxygen content increased (Figure 7a). The experimental
pressures have been at least 1,450 psi (100 bar), with a typical range of 1,800–2,000 psi (124–
138 bar), and the hourly space velocities (liquid or weight hourly space velocities documented in
Appendix A) have ranged from 0.1 to 0.35 h−1. Some experiments at NREL have included a non-
isothermal section prior to the final hydrotreating section, which resulted in longer problem-free
operations (tested during a ~600 h experiment without any disruptions or significant decline in
catalyst activity), very low oxygen contents (down to <0.01 wt %), enhanced hydrogenation, and
yielding cycloalkanes as the main product group [13,60,65].

  Figure 7. Stand-alone hydrotreating of CFP oils: (a) Carbon efficiencies and product oxygen
contents for stand-alone hydrotreating of CFP oils. Data from [13,60,65,68,69,70,72]. (b) Impact of
 hydrotreating temperature on fuel fraction distribution. Data from [13].

 50

The jet-range fraction of the hydrotreated product has ranged from 39 to 53 wt % by distillation,
with gasoline-range product constituting most of the rest (26–36 wt %) [13,65]. Increasing the
hydrotreating temperature enhanced cracking and produced more gasoline and jet fractions, and
less diesel and residue fractions (Figure 7b) [13]. When only gasoline and diesel were the
intended targets, the gasoline fraction typically encompassed 40–50 wt % and diesel 39–45 wt %
of the hydrotreated product [60,69,70,72].

The gasoline-range product research octane numbers (RON) have been estimated at 59–83 based
on detailed hydrocarbon analysis by gas chromatography–mass spectrometry [68,70,72]. The
highest values have been reported for HZSM-5-based CFP oils, which produce aromatic
compounds. Diesel from isothermal hydrotreating had low cetane numbers (derived cetane
number or DCN) of 13–26 [68,70,72], whereas including the initial non-isothermal section for
hydrogenation was shown to increase the cetane number (indicated cetane number or ICN) to 45
[60]. While hydrogenation of aromatic compounds enhances diesel properties, it decreases the
gasoline fraction octane number. The gasoline-range product may also be utilized for producing
aromatics (e.g., benzene, toluene, xylene) or as solvents [65].

The distilled jet fractions from the experiments with a non-isothermal initial zone and final
isothermal zone at 385°C–400°C have been measured for selected aviation fuel properties
(ASTM D7566 finished fuel blend properties and D4054 Tier 1 properties) [13,65]. The jet
fraction met ASTM guidelines for simulated distillation, flash point, freezing point, density, and
viscosity. The heats of combustion were slightly lower than the guidelines for some samples
(41.8–42.5 vs. ≥42.8 MJ/kg), as was the ICN. However, this does not preclude products with
these properties from being approved as blendstocks. These properties can also be improved by
further hydrogenation by adjusting reactor operations. Cycloalkanes were the main compound
group (up to 91%) and further increasing their fraction enhances the heat of combustion and
cetane number.

Data from continuous co-hydroprocessing experiments are summarized in Appendix A, Table A-
2. These include co-hydrotreating with SRD [38,60, previously unpublished data from NREL]
and co-hydrotreating/co-hydrocracking with VGO [59] or a mixture of VGO and LCO
[previously unpublished data from NREL]. For co-hydrotreating with SRD, blend levels up to
30% have been tested under conditions resembling those in commercial operations: isothermal at
325°C–340°C (617°F–644°F), 800–1,200 psi (55–83 bar), 1 h−1. Notable is the effective
deoxygenation with oxygen contents typically below detection limits that has been achieved
under these conditions that are significantly milder (e.g., temperature) than those required for
stand-alone hydrotreating. This could be due to synergetic effects with the more hydrogen-rich
petroleum stream, or more dispersed reactive oxygenated molecules and lower oxygen flow per
catalyst active site.

 51

Figure 8. Impact of co-hydrotreating of CFP oil with SRD on simulated distillation. Data from [60].
Carbon-14 measurements suggest approximately 95% incorporation of carbon from the CFP oil
into the final product [38,60]. The lower hydrotreating temperature may contribute to the high
carbon efficiency, which exceeds most values reported for stand-alone hydrotreating. Close to
50% of the biogenic carbon has been reported in the gasoline range and 44% in the diesel range
[38]. This is similar to the distribution of products for stand-alone hydrotreating and suggests
that co-hydrotreating does not impact the cracking of CFP oil. Another study confirmed
increases in the gasoline range and also in the heavy fractions (Figure 8) [60]. The ICN values of
the diesel product have been in the range of 42–50; these represented decreases from the values
of SRD-only hydrotreating [60].

Unpublished data from NREL includes hydrotreating CFP oil with SRD at blend levels of 10%–
30% for the production of jet-range fuel; 15%–17% of the products boiled in the jet range, and
the majority of the product was in the heavy diesel range boiling above the jet fraction.
Compared to SRD hydrotreating, there was no statistically significant increase in the jet fraction.
The jet fraction density increased, viscosity decreased, and heat of combustion decreased as the
blend level increased, as would be expected based on stand-alone hydrotreating results.

Santosa et al. [59] co-hydroprocessed CFP oils with low oxygen contents of 13%–14.5% (db)
over sulfided NiMo catalyst at 400°C at blend levels of 6%–20%. The products had oxygen
contents below the detection limit (<0.5 wt %), and the blending did not reduce the
desulfurization or cracking of the VGO. The jet-range product constituted 23%–27%, and
residue was the largest fraction at 65%–73%. The fraction of gasoline and jet increased as the
blend level increased. It should be noted that these experiments were performed over a typical
hydrotreating catalyst, and this resulted in limited VGO cracking. The biogenic carbon
incorporation was not explicitly given in the paper, but significant fractions of biogenic carbon
were measured in the product.

 52

Previously unpublished data from NREL with 20% CFP oil and the remaining 80% of petroleum
(50/50 mixture of VGO/LCO) feed includes co-hydrotreating over sulfided NiMo, combined
with co-hydrocracking over NiW/zeolite. The jet fraction was significantly higher (41%) and
residue lower (25%), compared to the study by Santosa et al. [59], which utilized only a
hydrotreating catalyst. While the measured jet fuel properties were good, the experiment was of
short duration (118 h total, of which 69 h with VGO and 50 h for coprocessing).

Co-hydrotreating and co-hydrocracking of a different biointermediate (hydrothermal liquefaction
oil with 1.4 wt % O and 3.2 wt % S) at a blend level of 7.5% showed that higher temperatures
(by 8°C) were required during hydrocracking to obtain the same conversion as with VGO only
[73], and the same may be expected for CFP oil as well. However, only minor changes in the
quality, hydrocarbon type, or hydrogen consumption were reported.

 53

3.1.1 Properties of Jet Cuts From Hydroprocessed CFP Oil Compared to ASTM
 Specifications
Below are the measured properties of jet cuts from some NREL experiments, with additional
data available in the literature including in Griffin et al. [13], supporting the premise that
hydroprocessed CFP oils can meet stringent jet fuel specifications. Distillation strategies and
choice of cut points will determine the properties of the remaining cuts, and those may be
blended into the gasoline and diesel pools to the extent dictated by specifics of distillation
choices.

 Figure 9. (a) Data for jet cut from hydrotreated HZSM-5 CFP oil from Chen et al. [65] and
 Watanasiri et al. [74]. Carbon numbers are based on two-dimensional gas chromatography.
 Distillation data from Simdist D86 correlation. (b) Data for jet cut from hydrotreated and
hydrocracked fuel product with 20% HZSM-5 CFP oil co-hydroprocessed with 80% petroleum feed
  (50% LCO + 50% VGO). Carbon numbers are based on gas chromatography–vacuum ultraviolet
spectroscopy. Distillation data from Simdist D86 correlation. The format of the plots was adapted
 from literature; the grey regions represent the range of conventional jet fuels, i.e., POSFs 10264,
 10325, 10289; the red regions represent outside specification limits [75,76]. POSFs are numbers
 assigned by the U.S. Air Force Research Laboratory to identify specific batches of fuel.

 54

3.2 Hydroprocessing Conceptual Models
Hydroprocessing designs can be diverse and implemented differently at each refinery or for
stand-alone operations. Configuration choices and process parameters are dictated primarily by
the types of feedstock and products intended for the unit, as well as the design choices of the
technology provider. A summary of the ranges of key operating variables in petroleum refinery
hydroprocessing operations, hydrotreating (HT) and hydrocracking (HC) are presented in Table
28. Our choices of modeled operating parameters were dictated by bench-scale experiments, and
we later discuss modeled results in the context of the ranges shown in Table 28. The base
hydroprocessing models in Aspen Plus were designed for 75,000 barrels per day (BPD); our cost
spreadsheet is designed to adapt for evaluating other throughputs and we present results for other
scales.

3.2.1 Petroleum Refining Hydroprocessing Configurations
Hydroprocessing configurations vary, and several options are presented in the literature [17,78].
Most hydroprocessing units for gasoline, jet fuel, and diesel fuel production utilize fixed trickle-
bed reactors in which feedstocks and hydrogen are fed to the top of downflow reactors and
through packed catalyst beds [78,79]. The two primary process configurations for fixed-bed
hydroprocessing units are single-stage and two-stage; two-stage configurations typically allow
intermediate adjustments before the second stage, thus enabling targeted processing of specific
portions of product from the first stage for value enhancement. Figure 10 presents process flow
diagrams for the single and two-stage hydroprocessing unit configurations.

 55

Single-stage units (Figure 10a) apply one or multiple reactors in series within a single hydrogen
circulation loop. These units generally have lower capital costs, simpler operations, and smaller
footprint requirements compared to the more complex two-stage designs since there are fewer
reactors and associated process control loops. Thus, single-stage units may generally have less
operational flexibility for deep desulfurization/denitrification in the conversion of challenging
and diverse feedstocks. However, the effectiveness of any configuration depends on the
situational ability to meet key objectives for converting specific feedstocks and the ability to
allow operational adaptations with changes in feedstock properties.

 Figure 10. Simplified configurations for (a) single-stage and (b) two-stage hydroprocessing units

 56

Two-stage units (Figure 10b) are designed with separate reactor systems with hydrogen makeup
to each stage and interstage separation to remove impurities before the second stage, which often
includes aromatics saturation, isomerization and/or hydrocracking catalysts for property and
quality improvement. The advantages of two-stage units include higher levels of operational
flexibility and feedstock variability since there are two reactor systems available to reach quality
targets. However, these units are more capital intensive, and complex for operation and process
control.

A refiner’s decision between a single-stage and a two-stage hydroprocessing unit depends on
feedstock quality and variability, product specifications, investment budget, and refinery
objectives. Single-stage units are cost-effective for moderate processing needs, while two-stage
units are ideal for stringent fuel regulations and complex feedstocks. Flexibility may be
beneficial when processing CFP oils, but at this point we do not have enough data to determine
the most advantageous design decisions when processing or coprocessing CFP oils; meeting
product quality and maximizing desired products will guide those decisions and justify additional
investments for a two-stage system.

Our process models are based on single-stage configurations since most of the data available in
the literature so far is from once-through experiments. We added the capability to recycle the
total condensed liquid product (prior to fractionation) as a potential means for cooling the
reactor. This is achieved through intermediate injection of the cold liquid or by recycling the
liquid product into the front-end reactor feed to suppress reactivity of the total liquid fed into the
reactor. This analysis can be extended to other configurations when additional data becomes
available.

3.2.2 Generic Conceptual Configuration for Hydrotreating
We used a generic single-stage hydroprocessing configuration for the process model in this
study. While there are variations in single-stage configurations, a general setup will usually
include the units shown in Figure 11; this figure also reflects the setup of the Aspen Plus model.
The same process configuration is carried through for all feeds included in this study. For
hydrotreating (referred as HT in this report) we include SRD as the petroleum feedstock. The
surrogate model compound slates for the two CFP oils from CFP-A and CFP-B processes
(discussed in Section 2) are included in Appendix C. The SRD surrogate model compound slate
is included in Appendix D. This hydroprocessing configuration was modified from the
configuration presented in the 2015 design report [18]; petroleum feedstocks and coprocessing
were not included in that report. The following description includes parameters common for all
cases, with additional specifics for select cases included in separate tables in the following
subsections. Note that many of these parameters are subsequently varied as part of sensitivity
analysis to assess their impacts. Stream summaries from the process models are included in
Appendix L for bookend cases with 100% CFP oil and 100% petroleum feed.

 57

 Figure 11. Conceptual design setup for hydrotreating (HT) configuration. Not all reactor cooling
 options shown may be exercised within the same setup.
Figure 11 shows the cold liquid feed stream going through a feed-effluent exchanger to preheat
the feed while cooling the hot reactor effluent. The preheated feed, mixed with hydrogen and
liquid recycle (if that option is chosen) is then further heated to the desired reactor inlet
temperature in a furnace. The mix of feed and hydrogen then enters the hydrotreating reactor.
The hydrotreating reactor includes inter-bed cooling via hydrogen and/or product liquid recycle
quench in the various cases presented later; in the model, both these inter-bed streams are cooled
to 43°C (110°F) before entering the reactor. The reactor specifications include an inlet pressure
of 1,045 psia (72 bara), and an inlet temperature of 340°C (644°F). Three beds are included, with
a 22°C (40°F) temperature rise allowed in each bed, by design. The reactor system outlet
pressure is 1,015 psia (70 bara). Stoichiometric reactions with associated conversions are
included in the model, with adjustments made for every case to achieve a consistent H:C ratio of
~1.8 in the total liquid (that includes all distillation cuts) fuel product and a 90% CFP oil
hydroprocessing carbon efficiency. In the process model the reactor includes three beds with
options for inter-bed injection of hydrogen and/or liquid recycle for cooling. The reactor
products after initial cooling in the feed-effluent exchanger go through another cooler which is
designed to generate steam in the process model. This is the source of steam coproducts at
different pressures included in the economic and sustainability analysis. Four steam pressure
options are included in the model: 15, 150, 350, and 900 psig (1, 10, 24, and 62 barg); the
amounts of steam at each pressure are dictated by the temperature-enthalpy distribution during
the cooling process. Wash water is then introduced, followed by an air cooler that cools the
stream to 60°C (140°F) and water cooler that further cools the stream to 43.3°C (110°F). The
cooled streams are separated in a high-pressure (HP) flash drum at 995 psia (68.6 bara), with
organic liquid, aqueous sour water, and a gaseous stream recovered. The gases are sent to an
amine scrubber for the removal of acid gases before being recycled to the reactor; a portion of

 58

the gas stream is then purged with the amount of purge determined by the target proportion of
hydrogen at the reactor inlet (80 mol %). The main gas stream (non-purged portion) is sent to a
recycle compressor with makeup hydrogen added to cover chemical hydrogen consumption and
purge losses. In some cases, the hydrogen purity remains above the 80 mol % design
specification, and a minimal amount of purge is included in those cases. The purge stream is sent
to a pressure swing adsorption (PSA) unit to recover hydrogen for reuse in the process. The PSA
specification is set to recover 85% of the hydrogen and the recovered hydrogen is accounted
within the model boundary to determine the net hydrogen consumption. The purge gas from the
PSA, along with gases recovered from the subsequent low-pressure (LP) flash unit and the gas
stream from final product distillation, are claimed as fuel gas coproducts. For simplicity, no
liquefied petroleum gas product/coproduct is included in the model. The organic liquid stream
from the HP flash unit is sent to a LP flash unit at 55 psia where additional light species are
recovered in the gas phase as a fuel gas product, as mentioned above. The liquid product is sent
for distillation, with the option of recycling a portion of this organic liquid product for reactor
heat management after pumping it to the higher reactor pressure.

3.2.3 Product Distillation
For simplicity of modeling, two distillation columns are included with three liquid fuel product
cut ranges: gasoline, jet, and diesel/heavies. The jet portion is maximized in the model, based on
the earlier discussion about potential future demand growth. Emphasis is not placed on the cuts
because they can be altered based on fuel demand, provided they meet specifications; a total
liquid fuel product with an H:C ratio of ~1.8 is produced and the goal of this analysis is to
understand process implications of meeting this criterion using various petroleum and CFP oil
feed options. Jet fuel properties are emphasized in Section 3.1 because of stringent requirements
and projected future demand. The properties of other cuts, gasoline and diesel/heavies will be
altered depending on the distillation strategy. Section 3.1 also provided references for gasoline
and diesel cut properties measured after some CFP oil hydrotreating experiments where a jet cut
was not included in the product slate. We have ongoing efforts to match product qualities and
fuel properties with experimental results [74,80] as more data become available, but that was not
a focus of the modeling work for this report. Thus, one should loosely interpret the proportions
of modeled cuts presented in Appendix K and product stream flows in Appendix L because they
do not reflect the experimental distillation properties reported in Section 3.1.

The liquid product after the LP flash unit is preheated via heat recovery from the distillation
products, followed by a preheat furnace. The feed temperature for column 1 is set at 450°F, and
it has 20 theoretical stages with feed entering above stage 6. The column overhead pressure is set
at 35 psia. The feed temperature for column 2 is 561°F, with no additional heating when the
bottoms product of column 1 is sent to column 2. Column 2 has 20 theoretical stages, with feed
entering above stage 16. The gases recovered from the overhead of column 1 are claimed as a
fuel gas coproduct. The gasoline cut is the top product of column 1, while jet and diesel/heavies
respectively are top and bottom products of column 2. As mentioned earlier, the H:C of the total
liquid product was ~1.8 for all cases in order to ensure similar product qualities across all cases.
The product cuts in the model were based on boiling points and not tied to quality requirements
for each cut. Design specifications in the RADFRAC blocks in Aspen Plus were used to get
rough product cut distributions. Column 1 specifications included: (i) 5 mole % recovery of
model compounds DECALIN + D-DECALN + L-NC10 + P-C10H20 + P-IC4CY6 in the
bottoms product, (ii) 2 mole % recovery of model compounds DIMDODEC + D-DIMDOD, P-

 59

3C3CY6 + P-NC15 + P-C14H20 in the top product. Column 2 specifications included: (i) 0.05
mole % recovery of model compounds DECALIN + D-DECALN + L-NC10 + P-C10H20 + P-
IC4CY6 in the bottoms product, (ii) 0.5 mole % recovery of model compounds MEFLOREN +
D-NC18 + P-C17H26 + L-NC18 in the top product. Specified recoveries are based on feeds to
each of the columns. Model compound names corresponding to the component IDs listed above
are included in Appendix D, Table D-6.

3.2.4 Generic Configuration for Hydrotreating Followed by Hydrocracking
A similar single-stage configuration (Figure 12) was adopted for a setup with pre-hydrotreating
followed by hydrocracking (referred as HT+HC in this report) for stand-alone and coprocessing
of CFP oil (with petroleum feed comprised of 50 vol % LCO + 50 vol % VGO). This
configuration was adopted from one of many options presented in the literature [78]. We used
this setup for the process model after collecting data from preliminary experiments to show that
this can be a potentially viable approach to produce fuels, including in the jet range [61]; these
experiments are mentioned in the discussion in Section 3.1 and some of the results reported in
Appendix A. The general description for this configuration is mostly identical to the
hydrotreating configuration described above, except for higher pressures in the reactor section of
the process and the inclusion of two reactors in series with different catalysts for pre-
hydrotreating and subsequent hydrocracking. Commercial catalysts, sulfided NiMoSx/Al2O3 for
hydrotreating and NiW/zeolite for hydrocracking, were used in NREL experiments [61].

 Figure 12. Hydroprocessing in a pretreat followed by hydrocracking (HT+HC) conceptual
 configuration (adapted from Hsu and Robinson [78]). Not all reactor cooling options shown may
 be exercised within the same setup.
The reactor inlet pressure was 2,045 psia (141 bara), with the HP flash unit at 1,995 psia (137.5
bara). The liquid product train pressure downstream of the HP flash unit is consistent with the

 60

hydrotreating configuration. The stoichiometric reactor model configuration and the product
specification (H:C ratio of ~1.8 in the total liquid fuel products) were maintained, consistent with
the hydrotreating model, with additional reactions added to capture hydrogenation and cracking
of LCO/VGO. Surrogate model compounds for representing LCO/VGO are listed in Appendix
D.

For the reactor system we duplicated the cost for the hydrotreating and hydrocracking reactors,
i.e., in this configuration we used two separate, but identical, reactor costs as shown in Appendix
B, Appendix H, and the HT+HC cases in subsequent Appendices I and K. Note that the reactor
cost estimates are adjusted for higher pressures.

3.2.5 Quench Systems
Heat and catalyst temperature management are some of the biggest constraints in
hydroprocessing systems and usually impact end-of-life and major maintenance decisions. With
the introduction of a considerably higher proportion of heteroatoms (oxygen in CFP oils) than
are present in most petroleum-derived feedstocks, careful design and operations decisions need
to be made if CFP oil is introduced in existing hydroprocessing systems that were originally
designed for petroleum feedstocks. Greater adjustments will be necessary for systems designed
to handle relatively lighter and hydrogen-saturated (e.g., paraffinic) feedstocks like SRD that
require less hydroprocessing, because there is lower hydrogen uptake for SRD compared to
systems with heavier and less hydrogen-saturated feedstocks like LCO/VGO that consume
significantly more hydrogen (Table 28). In other words, refinery systems that already have high
hydrogen consumption may be more adaptable to the high-hydrogen-consumption scenarios
associated with the hydroprocessing of CFP oils.

Quench system designs that include the injection of cold hydrogen between reactor beds are
widely used and have been studied for some time [81]. This design is included in both Figure 11
and Figure 12. Another option is to recycle and inject a portion of the post-hydroprocessed liquid
in between the reactor beds, depicted by dashed lines in Figure 11 and Figure 12 [82]. The post-
hydroprocessed liquid can also be included with the front-end feed stream, thus reducing
processing needs and associated heat of reaction for the overall feed when it includes post-
processed recycle. We considered both options in this study, with the model designed for either
(a) hydrogen or (b) liquid recycle as the manipulated variable to control reactor temperatures.
When inter-bed injection of recycled liquid is already included within a reactor system design,
there may be significant additional flexibility to adapt the control system to handle diverse feeds
with different exothermic behavior. Inter-bed liquid recycle injection can provide additional heat
removal compared to introducing with the front-end liquid feed because the cold recycle liquid
can further absorb heat from the reactor products through sensible temperature gain and
vaporization within the reactor. However, any liquid recycle option will typically entail capacity
curtailment for fresh feed. In many petroleum refining configurations processing heavier
feedstocks such as LCO/VGO, the liquid recycle stream is a heavier cut after distillation that
requires further cracking; that is not the case in our simplified conceptual design, and we only
included a split stream from the total liquid product prior to fractionation.

Hydrogen addition alone can also be an option for cooling when inter-bed liquid recycle cannot
be considered. When processing oxygenated feedstocks like CFP oil, that configuration will
typically also require either capacity curtailment via liquid product recycle to the front end or

 61

equipment debottlenecking to handle the requirement of higher gas flow rates. In general, the
introduction of CFP oils into petroleum units will require higher hydrogen flows for both heat
management via inter-bed cold H2 injection and for satisfying the reaction stoichiometry, while
maintaining appropriate hydrogen partial pressures.

Reactors may be designed to allow both hydrogen and liquid insertion between beds [83]. The
process model includes that dual option but allows only one of the two flows for temperature
control in any given configuration. When hydrogen is used for temperature control the liquid
recycle fraction can be specified in the model. When liquid recycle is used for temperature
control, the hydrogen concentration at the reactor outlet can be specified for modeling purposes;
during actual operations, hydrogen supply will align based on process operations and reaction
requirements, and the need for modifications will be dictated by the adequacy of reactor and
process performances toward achieving production and catalyst maintenance goals.

3.2.6 Hydrogen Purity
This study includes two makeup hydrogen purities, 99 mol % and 85 mol %, to assess their
impact on process parameters; the chosen purity level is reported with the results for the various
cases (and included in the code for each case, discussed later). The lower purity makeup is
common in refining operations, especially for relatively low hydrogen demand systems, such as
SRD hydrotreating. Higher purity makeup gas can help reduce purge and may affect other
parameters like recycled gas volume (depending on factors such as the temperature control
strategy); this can become an important tool during process modifications as the hydrogen
demand increases with changes in feedstock properties. A PSA unit can be added to increase the
makeup hydrogen purity and can be a stand-alone retrofit outside the main hydroprocessing unit.

Note that we maintained hydrogen purity of 80 mol % in the gas feed into the reactor for most of
the cases reported, and this variable is controlled in the model by manipulating the quantity of
treat gas purge. In some cases (examples available in Appendix K), the hydrogen purity into the
reactor was >80% when 99 mol % hydrogen makeup is introduced in the relatively lower
hydrogen demand processes.

3.2.7 Space Velocity Adjustments and Catalyst Requirement
Experimental data in Appendix A shows the range of space velocities from available
experimental CFP oil hydroprocessing results. Carefully planned parametric studies are
necessary for evaluating catalyst performance for both stand-alone and coprocessing
configurations. Long-term decisions should not be based on short-duration bench-scale
assessments, and all operating variables including reactor temperature and pressure, hydrogen
partial pressure, and space velocities need to be varied to quantify their impacts. However, long-
term pilot tests may be expensive, and proper experimental design at different scales is important
to maximize learnings to enable successful scale-up. As discussed in Section 3.1, short-duration
CFP oil stand-alone- and co-hydroprocessing experiments at NREL and Pacific Northwest
National Laboratory using catalysts that are commercially relevant for petroleum feedstocks
have identified many potential challenges. Bench-scale experiments have also helped understand
some potential mitigation strategies for longer duration operations, such as the inclusion of an
initial non-isothermal reaction zone when processing CFP oils and other reactive oxygenated
feedstocks.

 62

While space velocities necessary to achieve deoxygenation and hydrogenation to meet product
specifications may be directly inferred in the case of stand-alone CFP oil hydroprocessing at a
chosen set of conditions, the parameter space and combinations for understanding the limits
during co-hydroprocessing are more extensive and include various possible feed options and
compositions. Space velocity at lower levels of CFP oil cofeed may need no adjustments if the
space velocity for the unit has spare capacity for meeting stringent specifications, e.g., for ultra-
low-sulfur diesel. While the CFP oil may be converted to on-spec fuel product, there may be
competing or inhibitory effects of CFP oil on petroleum feedstock hydroprocessing that may
cause changes in hydrodesulfurization (HDS) and hydrodenitrogenation (HDN). Overall, there
may be inhibitory impacts and competition among HDS, HDN, and hydrodeoxygenation, and
those effects need to be understood through experimental studies. Although this phenomenon
was pronounced for biocrude from hydrothermal liquefaction from waste (sewage sludge and
food waste in this reference) [72] and not for CFP oils, this should remain a consideration as the
quality of CFP oils may vary in the future and low-sulfur and low-nitrogen CFP oils may not
always be produced from different biomass sources. Similarly, it was noticed that the product
H:C ratio for VGO/LCO hydroprocessing was lowered in the presence of 20 vol % CFP oil [61].
Given these impacts it is necessary to assess coprocessing situations individually, although the
template of such parametric studies may become immediately evident for any specific
configuration and feedstock option. Additional adjustment factors may need to be added, e.g., if
catalyst activity-inhibiting CO gas is present and accumulates in the recycle system (CO is
discussed separately); there may also be inhibitory effects from specific components or
impurities in the CFP oil, depending on the source.

Given this uncertainty and the absence of extensive parametric experimental studies, we chose a
general approach to cover the space velocity uncertainty. Reaction demands on the catalyst can
be estimated from the chemical hydrogen demand in a hydroprocessing system. Our SRD model
quantified a chemical hydrogen consumption of approximately 615 scf/bbl. We assumed a
weight hourly space velocity (WHSV) of 2 h−1 for this system following general ranges and
guidelines from literature (Table 28); catalyst loading for the remaining cases were based on
prorating the chemical hydrogen consumption with this SRD system. Thus, as an example, if the
chemical hydrogen consumption is double of 615 scf/bbl, at 1,230 scf/bbl, the WHSV required
for the reactor system is assumed to be half of 2 h−1, at 1 h−1. This information is included in the
result summaries provided for key cases later in this report, and for all the cases in Appendix K.
Based on the experimental discussion in Section 3.1, we assume the use of commercial catalysts;
catalyst unit cost assumptions are stated later under operating costs. For the HT+HC
configuration (Figure 12), we assumed for reactor and catalyst costing purposes that the space
velocity calculated based on the total chemical hydrogen consumption was doubled for each
reactor, since the reactions are spread across the two reactors. Note that the WHSVs reported in
Appendix K include metrics based on both fresh feed and total feed (with recycle); the metric
based on the total feed can be indicative of deviations from the status quo of petroleum feedstock
processing when units are considered for repurposing with the introduction of CFP oils or other
oxygenated feedstocks, along with any specific proportion of product recycle.

3.2.8 Operating Temperature and Pressure
Typical reported hydroprocessing temperatures range from 300°C to 400°C (572°F to 752°F),
with more severe cracking operations extending to 450°C (842°F), as shown in Table 28. The
units are also designed to limit temperature increases (ΔT) within specifications to optimize

 63

performance and catalyst maintenance from the start to end of run with a catalyst load. The
design operating pressure of the unit is determined based on the required severity for the
feedstock being handled, with higher design pressures (and higher associated H2 partial
pressures) applied to more difficult heavier feeds. These reactor temperature profile and ΔT are
changed during the lifetime of a catalyst load to achieve reactor performance goals.

As previously mentioned, this study is focused on understanding process impacts with specified
reactor performance but does not include predictive reactor behavior. Thus, the specifications of
reactor temperature, ΔT, and pressure specifications do not impact the model yields and product
quality. We picked values within the ranges for hydroprocessing units (Table 28) and then varied
them in sensitivity cases to understand process and cost impacts of choosing different design and
operating temperatures and pressures. The impacts of varying model yields are considered in
separate sensitivity cases.

The base pressures chosen for HT (Section 3.2.2) and HT+HC (Section 3.2.4) reactor inlets are
1,045 psia (72 bara) and 2,045 psia (141 bara), respectively. The base reactor inlet temperature
was 340°C (644°F), with a ΔT of 22.2°C (40°F). Note that this assumption was maintained in
both configurations. In practice the two reactors in the HT+HC configuration will likely be
operated at different temperatures to facilitate different target chemistries in the two reactors, as
was during the NREL experiments mentioned in Section 3.1 and in Appendix A (Table A-2),
with hydrotreating at 385°C (725°F) and hydrocracking at 400°C (752°F) [61].

3.2.9 CO and CO2 Formation From Oxygenated Species
As mentioned earlier, the primary advantage of hydroprocessing over FCC processing of CFP
oils is higher carbon efficiencies because hydrodeoxygenation (e.g., removal of oxygen as water)
is the primary mode of oxygen removal, versus the formation of CO and CO2 in an FCC where
oxygen removal is accompanied by carbon loss. However, the formation of CO and CO2 to some
extent during hydroprocessing is a possibility because of the mix of compounds present in the
process. Hydroprocessing results for CFP oils from NREL and Pacific Northwest National
Laboratory have so far reported no measured CO formation, and small amounts of CO2 were
reported in some cases [59,84]. Since an acid gas removal system will already be included in the
design for H2S removal, CO2 accumulation may not be a concern. However, differences in amine
reactivity with H2S and CO2 may need to be assessed based on specific situations. One must also
be mindful of potential concerns such as formation of carboxylic acid and ammonium carbonate
salts; associated corrosion may require reevaluation of metallurgy in the reactor effluent cooling
sections and wash water requirements to avoid salt precipitation [85]. On the other hand, if CO is
formed, it can potentially accumulate in the gas recycle loop; in that case, increasing the
proportion of purge gas may be one of the primary options to reduce CO concentrations. The
literature indicates that CO formation can inhibit catalyst activity, with one source showing
reduced hydrotreatment activity with straight-run gas oil. They reported 5% decrease in
saturation, 4%–15% decrease in HDS, 5%–10% decrease in HDN activities upon spiking the
hydrogen feed with approximately 1.4 vol % CO [86]. Other studies where CO2 and/or CO were
artificially introduced during gas oil hydrotreating also captured inhibitory effects on HDS,
HDN, and hydrogenation [87,88]. However, as noted, we do not expect significant levels of CO
to accumulate during CFP oil hydroprocessing, based on bench-scale results reported; acid gas
removal included in the gas recycle loop can reduce CO2 concentrations.

 64

3.2.10 Water Wash in Reactor Effluent Cooling and Acid Gas Removal
Salts from sulfur, nitrogen, and chlorine (from reaction with hydrogen) have the potential to form
highly concentrated and corrosive solutions as hydroprocessing reactor effluents are cooled and
condensed to liquids. Therefore, water wash is often required to (1) maintain adequate free water
in the effluent train and (2) control the concentration of salts in the condensed aqueous phase to
non-corrosive levels. Based on industry guidance, the modeling targets include minimum 25%
free water at salt deposition temperature and maximum 2 wt % NH4HS. This target corresponds
to approximately 4.7 wt % ammonium bisulfate (NH4)HSO4; the goal is to maintain this
concentration well below the corrosion limit of 8 wt %. Water produced in hydrodeoxygenation
reactions contributes to the effluent wash water, thus reducing the additional water wash makeup
injection required to meet the modeled targets. It is noted that CFP oil from woody biomass is
low in sulfur, nitrogen, and chlorine, and coprocessing CFP oils of this type is not expected to
aggravate concerns of salt formation, unless the biomass feedstock for CFP oil production is
switched from a woody source. Operational adjustments may be necessary even if the changes
are small and the existing design is capable of accommodating those changes. As mentioned in
the previous section, concerns about carboxylic acid and ammonium carbonate salts may need to
be addressed if a significant amount of CO2 is formed during hydroprocessing. Acid gas and
ammonia separations were modeled using simple separator (SEP) blocks in Aspen Plus,
equimolar amounts of NH3 and H2S are reported in the stream summary tables (Appendix L) to
represent NH4HS, with additional H2S removal reported in a separate stream. For simplicity,
complete removal of NH3, H2S and CO2 is included in the SEP blocks. Operating cost estimation
basis and amine makeup for acid gas removal are included in Section 3.3.1. Capital costs for
amine scrubbers are included in the equipment cost estimates provided later.

3.2.11 Sour Wastewater Impacts
Sour water is a product from hydroprocessing due to water in the feed streams, produced water
from deoxygenation, and supplemental wash water. There is a cost of wastewater treatment
included in the TEA basis. However, requirements or processing strategies for treatment of new
wastewater species introduced with CFP oil is not addressed in the scope of this work. It is
assumed that the existing refinery water treatment facility could meet water discharge permit
specifications without incurring major capital or operating costs. Cost assumption per gallon of
wastewater treatment is included under operating costs (Table 30).

Phenolic compounds may be a major concern for treatment of wastewater from bio-oil upgrading
processes where near-complete deoxygenation is not achieved. Phenols are organic acids formed
when an aromatic phenyl group (C6H5-) bonds to a hydroxyl group. The most common example
is carbolic acid, more commonly known simply as phenol. When phenol dissolves in water it
dissociates into a hydrogen ion and phenolate ion or phenoxide, resulting in an acidic and
potentially corrosive and toxic solution. With the near-complete deoxygenation requirement for
the production of jet fuel, it may be expected that most of the phenolic compounds will be
saturated and/or deoxygenated at the exit of the hydroprocessing reactors, thus alleviating this
concern regarding significant discharge of phenolic compounds. This may however require
additional wastewater treatment considerations if more phenols partition into the aqueous phase
when deliberate partial deoxygenation is one of the goals, e.g., for potential production of marine
fuels that can tolerate some fuel oxygen [61,89], but more experimental data is necessary before

 65

drawing conclusions. Partial deoxygenation during hydroprocessing is not a strategy discussed in
this report.

3.2.12 Additional Assumptions for Refinery Integration Scenarios
There are several additional assumptions for refinery integration as outlined below:

Amine Systems: The refinery provides lean amine and regeneration of rich amine from existing
facility capabilities to the hydroprocessing unit.

Acid Gas: Any CO2 yielded from CFP oil processing in the refinery will be captured by the
amine scrubbing system on the hydrogen recycle loop of the hydroprocessing unit. Incremental
CO2 may impact the apparent capacity of the sulfur recovery unit. However, the impact is
expected to be minimal and offset by reduced sulfur load to the refinery with CFP oil processing.
Depending on the biogenic feedstock’s chemical composition, larger proportions of CO2 may be
produced during coprocessing, and the CO2 may not be completely removed without augmenting
amine system capacity, as may be the case during hydroprocessed esters and fatty acids (HEFA)
coprocessing [90].

Catalysts: Any specially designed catalyst technologies for optimization of CFP oil upgrading, if
necessary, are assumed to negligibly impact catalyst unit cost; we assumed no changes to
commercial catalysts for this report. Increased catalyst consumption for CFP oil upgrading is
addressed through space velocity adjustments discussed previously. However, frequent catalyst
changeouts can negatively impact the on-stream factor of the unit, as discussed in Section
3.4.4.14.

Product Quality and Blending: The fuel properties of products from CFP oil are assumed to meet
quality specifications of fossil derived equivalents after adequate hydroprocessing. This aspect is
discussed in Section 3.1 and Section 3.2.3.

3.2.13 Feed System Modification
NREL bench-scale experiments and input from industry partners have led to the conceptual CFP
oil feeding strategy proposed in Figure 13. The design aims to minimize undesired thermal
polymerization reactions in CFP oil during feeding into a heated system, which leads to coking,
plugging, and other operational problems. The CFP oil feed system includes dedicated tankage,
pumps, static mixers with hydrogen addition, and a small stabilization reactor upstream of the
main hydroprocessing reactor as insurance for handling higher oxygen content CFP oils. This
proposed design may also address common mixing and miscibility challenges due to non-
homogeneity of hydrocarbon and oxygenate mixtures. The costs of these systems are included in
our estimates. More extensive designs of stabilization units may be warranted based on
experimental data for specific feedstocks. The current system is based on minimally ensuring
that the CFP oil is in the presence of hydrogen, in a catalytic environment within enhanced
piping, as it gets heated while being fed into the reactor system; this can prevent polymerization
and plugging as has been demonstrated in bench-scale experiments. Catalyst costs are not
estimated separately for this section, with an assumption that the catalyst needs for this section
can be absorbed with the catalyst cost for the main hydroprocessing reactors since our
assumption is that significant hydroprocessing will not occur in this section beyond avoidance of
polymerization. The equipment shown in Figure 13 are listed in Appendix B.

 66

 Figure 13. CFP oil feed design for upgrading by hydroprocessing (feed filters not shown)

3.3 Hydroprocessing Process Economics and Sustainability Analysis
The general framework for the estimation of process economics and sustainability was described
in Section 1.2. Additional specifics for hydroprocessing are stated in this section. As noted in
Section 1.2.2, capital costs for hydroprocessing were based on KBR estimates that were further
correlated for scaling by NREL; details are provided in Appendix B.

3.3.2 Carbon Intensity Quantification
Carbon intensity (CI) calculations are coupled with the TEA framework for this analysis. The
NREL analysis team, in consultation with Argonne National Laboratory, incorporated
greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions factors based on GREET [27] for modeled process inputs and
coproducts, including petroleum and CFP oil feedstocks, to enable direct CI calculations for the
yielded products. Coproducts, steam and fuel gas, are accounted via displacement credits. An
example is provided in Section 3.4.2.2 to illustrate the methodology and show our assumptions
for emission factors.

 69

reader understand the modeled impacts of various parameters.

The example Case ID 12120211 reflects 80 vol % SRD + 20 vol % CFP-A oil hydrotreating with
99% purity hydrogen makeup supply. Reactor cooling is achieved using inter-bed hydrogen
quench for temperature control (H-TC) and aided by 20% inter-bed recycle of the total
condensed liquid product. The total capacity of the unit is 75k BPD, with 20 vol % or 15k BPD
liquid product recycle and the balance of 60k BPD fresh feed.

Table 33 shows the feed information to the unit. It includes 20 vol % CFP-A oil and 80 vol %
SRD in fresh feed. This case includes temperature control using inter-bed hydrogen injection and
also has 20 vol % inter-bed liquid recycle to help reactor heat management. In the process model,
the total feed to the reactor is 75k BPD, with 20 vol % recycled product liquid (15k BPD) and 80
vol % (60k BPD) fresh feed. Of the 60k BPD, 12k BPD (20 vol %) is CFP-A oil and 48k BPD
(80 vol %) is SRD.

Table 34 shows modeled product information for the case. As mentioned elsewhere, although
distillation is included in the model to provide a nominal idea of approximate distributions
among the three potential products: gasoline, jet, and diesel/heavies, the cuts may be changed
based on fuel demand and quality specifications. The primary criteria for all the hydroprocessing
cases are (1) the removal of all oxygen and (2) having an H:C molar ratio of ~1.8 in the total fuel
product (including all three cuts). The units of barrels gasoline, jet, and diesel equivalents (BGE,
BJE, and BDE) are provided to allow the reader to gauge the total energy content of the products
in different units. These units are derived from GGE, GJE, and GDE mentioned earlier by using
a conversion of 42 gal/bbl. Information about weight precents in each cut in the model are
included but not emphasized as a strict metric for this report.

Table 35 shows information related to hydroprocessing and catalyst assumptions. It includes the
SRD hydrotreating chemical hydrogen consumption of 615 scf/bbl and WHSV of 2 h−1 used as
the basis for prorating the WHSV for the cases presented. For the HT+HC cases (Figure 12) the
calculated WHSV is doubled because the reactions occur over two reactors. In this case the
hydrogen consumption increases to 1,316 scf/bbl of total feed (including recycle) and the

 71

capacity of fresh feed is reduced from 75k BPD to 60k BPD (with 20% liquid recycle). As a
result, the WHSV based on fresh feed is reduced from 2 h−1 for the 100% SRD case to 0.75 h−1.

Recycling liquid product into intermediate points of the reactor helps cool the reactor, as is the
case here with 20% liquid product recycle. As discussed later, the cooling advantage is lost if the
full recycle is fed to the front end of the reactor, as may be the case when reusing a reactor that is
not already equipped for inter-bed recycle of liquid. In both inter-bed and front-end liquid
recycle configurations the recycled product liquid reduces reactivity of the total feed, thus
reducing the hydroprocessing exotherm; the recycled liquid also helps maintain the liquid flow
profile in the reactor at the designed flow rate, even though fresh feed rate is curtailed.

Table 36 includes metrics for hydrogen use in the process. In our economic spreadsheet,
chemical hydrogen consumption in standard cubic feet (scf) of hydrogen per barrel (bbl) of fresh
feed to the unit is used to prorate the WHSV and calculate the catalyst load, based on the stream
flow rate of the fresh feed for scaling (the calculation may also be performed using a total feed
basis since this involves simple and appropriate algebraic adjustments). The purity of hydrogen
used in each case is shown. The hydrogen purity at the reactor inlet (modeled value) is also
shown. In some cases, the usual specification of 80 mol % cannot be met with a purge, especially
when the makeup hydrogen purity is 99 mol % and hydrogen consumption is relatively low, such
as during 100% SRD hydroprocessing. For the petroleum base case with 100% SRD
hydrotreating (case 12201011) we use 85 mol % purity hydrogen makeup.

Table 37 includes a summary of TEA results and sustainability metrics. The MFSP is calculated
on an energy basis of the total fuel products and is shown in GGE. GJE and GDE values are also
shown to allow a quick estimate of prices of different fuels from this process, with the simple
assumption that costs will be prorated on an energy content basis. In practice volumetric units are
used by refiners; the GGE, GJE, and GDE units are included here to provide information using
approximate average equivalent volumes of different fuels while avoiding potential errors in
estimating densities. It is noted that other factors, including market forces, determine fuel retail
prices. Refinery operations are adjusted based on market prices and delta yield analyses, which
are not included in this analysis. The values shown are for 75k BPD plants, which is the total
capacity of the base process model. Our economic spreadsheets are enabled to assess TEA
impacts with scaled capital costs estimated at other lower capacities; this is discussed later under
sensitivity cases.

 73

Liquid feed is the dominant cost, but as expected and shown later, hydrogen costs increase as the
proportion of CFP oil increases. Capital costs will also increase relative to existing petroleum
processing units if CFP oil is introduced without appropriately curtailing feed capacity to allow
reuse of existing capacities of unit operations. Further discussion about constraints is included
later in this report.

An MFSP estimate with the reuse of existing paid-up capital for a 100% petroleum feedstock
unit with expenses for subsequent modifications is also projected for each case. In this context,
the 100% petroleum SRD hydrotreating base case is used for calculating capital savings when
existing paid-up equipment is repurposed for CFP oil hydrotreating or co-hydrotreating
(configuration shown in Section 3.2.2). The base case number used for estimating repurposed
capital in each case is included in Appendix K. The base GHG emissions and corresponding
reductions are also based on the 100% SRD hydrotreating case. There is a corresponding
petroleum hydroprocessing baseline for LCO/VGO feed in the HT+HC configuration for
assessing capital savings and GHG reduction in the other configuration (configuration shown in
Section 3.2.4).

3.4.2.2 Life Cycle Inventory and GHG Emissions Calculations
Table 38 includes the life cycle inventory and corresponding life cycle GHG emission
calculations for the example case above. The emission factors are based on R&D GREET 2023
[27] and are used for calculating GHG emissions for all the cases reported. The emission factors
are not repeated in following tables and only final calculated GHG emissions are presented for
subsequent results. For cases where exact emission factors are not available for a specific
situation, e.g., for SRD, LCO, and VGO after these intermediate refinery streams are produced,
but before they are hydroprocessed to fuels, a small adjustment was made based on available
information for hydroprocessed SRD to remove the estimated GHG emissions from
hydroprocessing, as calculated by our model, thus matching the overall diesel baseline life cycle
GHG emissions modeled with R&D GREET 2023. Specifically, the R&D GREET 2023 value of
15.629 kg CO2e/MMBtu for diesel was adjusted to 13.9066 kg CO2e/MMBtu in Table 38 after
removing SRD hydroprocessing emissions estimated by our model. Note that hydroprocessing-
generated emissions are accounted via the life cycle inventory of our process models, and this
adjustment avoids double-counting. This is important for the other cases evaluated, because the
introduction of CFP oil changes the calculated GHG emissions for hydroprocessing, and those
emissions are evaluated using the life cycle inventory from the process model for each case. The
emissions associated with the production of untreated LCO and VGO streams were assumed
identical to SRD (on an energy basis) and also use the derived value for untreated SRD (13.9066
kg CO2e/MMBtu).
