54. Comment: Include the yields and heat content of the products in the results. That would be
 a good indicator of how the processing options change based on differing amounts of CFP
 oil and indirectly indicate hydrogen usage. Regulatory incentives for renewable fuels are not
 geared toward gasoline and instead favor sustainable aviation fuel (SAF) and renewable
 diesel. It will be useful to understand what products would really be maximized (e.g., SAF)
 and which would be minimized (low-octane gasoline).

 Response: As stated in the report, the included reactor model is not predictive and does not
 predict yields and properties of product fuel cuts. The amounts of distillation cuts reported in
 Appendix K are based on the same boiling cuts specifications for all the cases, as stated in
 Section 3.2.3. Mass flow of the total liquid product, along with weight fractions of each cut
 are reported along with the LHV of the liquid fuel product. LHVs are reported in barrels
 gasoline equivalent (BGE), with a 42-gal/bbl conversion used to convert GGE to BGE.
 Experimental distributions of cuts from bench-scale experiments are discussed in Section
 3.1, indicating that with sufficient hydrotreatment one can achieve 40-50 wt % yields of jet
 fuel from CFP oils. In this context it is pointed out that the jet fuel cuts after
 hydroprocessing 100% CFP-A and CFP-B oils, if certified for use as jet fuel based on fuel
 properties, can qualify as SAF because they achieve greater than 50% GHG reduction
 compared to corresponding petroleum-derived jet fuel, per criteria in current legislation [1].
 However, the CFP oils hydroprocessing pathway is not currently certified for acceptance as
 jet fuel, and more R&D and toward increased production along with associated testing is
 necessary before this conversion pathway can successfully integrate with jet fuel supplies.

55. Comment: In the previous draft version case for 20% CFP oil in the feedstock, the cost
 impact between 25k BPD and 75k BPD units was 4 cents per GGE and the impact from
 using an existing hydroprocessing unit was between 1-3 cents per GGE. One would have
 expected the MFSP to be much larger for new units, compared to the repurposed case.

 Response: Figure 28 in Section 3.4.4.12 shows similar results in the final report. As
 mentioned in the report, the impact of capital on the MFSP decreases when large volumes
 are processed, noting that the quantifications presented are based on our TEA model
 assumptions. One can see that the impact of capital costs, and hence the difference in the
 MFSP between new and repurposed units, increase significantly at the smaller scales of 2.4k
 BPD and 8k BPD shown in Figure 28.

56. Comment: Plots presented in the earlier draft switched between wt % and vol % for the
 proportion of CFP oil included during coprocessing. One should stick with vol % of CFP oil
 in the feed.

 296

57. Comment: In the previous draft version, explain the non-linearity in CI and hydrogen
 consumption when 10% increments of CFP oil were introduced in the cofeed with petroleum
 feedstocks.

 Response: In the current version, the modeled CI based on 10 vol % increments of CFP oil
 follows a linear trend (Figure 14 and Figure 15). The reason for the non-linearity in the
 previous version was because the plot in the previous version was presented using wt % CFP
 oil on the x-axis (instead of vol %). Since the analysis assumption includes constant total
 feed volume in these cases, and petroleum and CFP oil feeds differ significantly in their
 densities, the trend with total weight of the feed is not linear (when constant volume of feed
 is maintained). Hence the use of wt % on the x-axis results showed a nonlinear trend for CI
 (and hydrogen consumption) in the previous draft version.

58. Comment: In the previous draft, CI presented for the same case in two different figures
 with 20 % CFP oil did not match.

 Response: The reason for the difference is the same as indicated in the previous comment.
 One of the figures reported the value for 20 wt % CFP oil, while the other reported for 20
 vol % CFP oil. The values at the 20 wt % and 20 vol % differ because of differences in the
 densities of CFP oil and petroleum feedstocks. All figures in the final version use vol % CFP
 oil for consistency. Most of the values presented in the figures are also tabulated in
 Appendix K for the interested reader.

60. Comment: It may be useful to have a total heteroatoms vs. CFP oil content plot. This can be
 a surrogate for hydrogen consumption.

 Response: We agree that CFP oil heteroatoms content is an indicator of hydrogen
 consumption. There is an additional key component in the calculation, the H:C molar ratio
 (in the feed and products) that will impact the hydrogen consumption and require the
 inclusion of multiple parameters to complete this plot. We presented two CFP oil cases
 (CFP-A and CFP-B) with different oxygen contents in the final report but did not include the
 mentioned plot. It can be derived for any specific choices of feed and their proportions.
 Once the petroleum feed (with its H:C ratio and constituent heteroatoms) and CFP oil feed
 (with its H:C ratio and constituent oxygen) are determined, the hydrogen consumption for
 achieving the product’s H:C ratio (along with removal of heteroatoms) will be dictated by
 reaction stoichiometry.

61. Comment: Include % GHG reduction to allow lookup of various credits associated with the
 % GHG reduction. E.g., if 50% GHG reduction is necessary to qualify as in SAF then one
 can extend the MFSP results to include those credits after accounting for credits based on

62. Comment: Color coding for different plant sections in the process flow diagram (in the
 previous draft) was confusing. Based on the color coding it seemed that repurposing an
 existing asset for dedicated CFP oil will require the addition of a new distillation train, but
 that coprocessing would not need the new distillation system. Unless the distillation unit
 would be shared between multiple different hydroprocessing units it seems like this cost
 should be the same for the two scenarios.

 Response: The color coding has been removed from the diagram (now Figure B-1). The
 reviewer is correct that the cost when repurposing distillation units should be similar
 irrespective of whether the unit is repurposed for stand-alone or coprocessing operations.
 Some of the costs incurred for modifications will be based on capacity changes, and any
 additional changes in product cuts. As mentioned in the main text, the FCI for each
 repurposed equipment was estimated by subtracting the cost of new equipment from the cost
 of the original unit being repurposed.

63. Comment: Is the reported MFSP an overall weighted average of the different products? If
 so, it should be described. It would be helpful to first discuss the overall yield structure of
 gases/gasoline/jet/diesel for the different feedstocks before jumping into the economic
 summary.

 Response: This is an important point, but since we don’t currently have enough information
 to develop predictive reactor models that can include interactions among various liquid feed
 mixtures in a hydroprocessing environment, we chose to present an overall MFSP for the
 entire liquid fuel product slate on an energy basis. The total liquid product slate has a H:C
 molar ratio of ~1.8 across all the cases (except for specific sensitivity cases varying the H:C
 parameter), because this helps make direct comparisons among the cases. The overall H:C
 ratio of ~1.8 was chosen to represent hydroprocessing to a point where one can consider
 distillation into different potentially viable fuel cuts and is convenient for interpreting
 process model results without being convoluted by incomplete or premature reactor model
 predictions of fuel properties and associated H:C ratios. Although we do report the different
 cuts for each case (in Appendix K), these cuts are purely distillation (boiling point) based,
 and the model compounds do not represent any detailed consideration of required quality
 specifications within individual cuts; hence we de-emphasize that aspect for this report and

 298

 focus on the overall MFSP on an energy basis. We have other ongoing work [9] to tie that
 aspect into future process models when more data becomes available.

64. Comment: Comment regarding the trend in the plot shown in Figure 30. One would expect
 a graph like this to have a local minimum at some optimum WHSV below which the yield is
 too poor and above which the capital costs and catalyst replacement costs are too high. This
 graph would imply one should go to a much higher WHSV.

 Response: As mentioned earlier, the reactor behavior is not predictive. The goal of this
 sensitivity case is to analyze cost impacts if more or less catalyst is necessary to achieve the
 same hydroprocessing performance (in this case, a fuel product H:C ratio of ~1.8). The
 reviewer’s observation can be illustrated if we develop a kinetic model that can be used to
 predict reactor behavior, including classification of product species to represent different
 fuel cuts.

 Response: In the final draft we provided information for all the cases that include both
 MFSPs and CI (in Appendix K). The interested reader may apply potential incentives based
 on the information provided. We did not present those calculations using specific incentives.
 The delta from the fossil baseline provided will allow the reader to easily calculate the cost
 differential, hence the incentives required for cost parity.

66. Comment: Include the CI for 0% CFP oil cases.

 Response: This has been included in the relevant plots and all values are included in
 Appendix K.

69. Comment: For bioliquid coprocessing, one should assume the cost of refinery modification
 increases with the level of bioliquid inserted. For HEFA coprocessing, typically 0-5% blend
 requires just a catalyst change, 5-15% blend requires additional changes, and for >20% it
 often seems that a 100% HEFA product unit may be more reasonable. Can one make that
 analogy here?

 Response: We thank the reviewer for providing these insights. A constraints-focused
 analysis is in our future plans. In this final version we already have the information
 necessary for such analysis, although not directly presented in that manner. Most of the
 tables and plots show gas flows with total liquid flow capacity maintained. The results show
 how some of the parameters start running into process constraints by exceeding what may be
 design allowances of the systems. Often ~15% allowances are included in equipment
 designs but will vary for specific situations; we did not include potential cost savings from
 design allowances because we did not want to make a general design allowance assumption
 applicable to all units. We present options for mitigating some of the constraints, e.g.,
 through liquid recycle or increasing hydrogen makeup purity. We feel we have presented
 information (along with details in Appendix K) along the lines of the reviewer’s request but
 can further specifically address this in subsequent publications since we already have the
 model framework to do so. Guidelines in this regard will depend on multiple parameters,
 including the quality (primarily oxygen content) of the CFP oil feed.

70. Comment: For HEFA hydroprocessing unit conversions to 100% biofeed, one typically
 sees a 60-80% reduction in unit throughput capacity compared to petroleum feed processing
 due to the generation of additional heat.

 Response: Thank you for this information. One can consider different parameters as the
 constraint that limits the capacity of the unit. For illustration, using the total catalyst load
 (included in Appendix K) as the parameter to approximate the necessary capacity reduction
 we can come up with approximate reductions for different feed combinations:
 Catalyst load for Catalyst load for Estimated
 Petroleum CFP Oil 100% Petroleum 100% CFP Oil Capacity
 Feed Feed for 75k BPD (lb) for 75k BPD (lb) Reduction
           SRD          CFP-A           549,791             6,829,318             92%
           SRD          CFP-B           549,019             4,374,350             87%
           LCO/VGO      CFP-A          2,054,530            6,812,546             70%
           LCO/VGO      CFP-B          2,054,471            4,353,285             53%
 Note that these reduction estimates do not include any other mitigation strategies like liquid
 recycle. In many cases, additional mitigation strategies will need to be deployed for practical
 implementations.

71. Comment: Coprocessing can mean different things to different people. Some people
 assume coprocessing means blending the liquids in the same storage tank, while generally

 300

 blending liquids in line to the first reactor or even providing separate feeds to the first
 reactor is much more likely.

 Response: In our case we assume providing separate feeds to the first reactor, as discussed
    in Section 3.2.13.

72. Comment: Include typical gas and liquid recycle ratios from literature in Table 28.

 Response: These values differ with reactors, feedstocks, and desired product cuts. We did
 not include these values in Table 28, but do include relevant information in Appendix K so
 the interested reader has these values for our modeled cases. Based on values presented by
 Gary et al. [10] one can derive a hydrogen recycle to consumption ratio range of 2.5 to 10.
 In our modeled single-stage configurations, the total liquid product is recycled to help
 maintain the total liquid feed rate to the reactor and provide reactor cooling. No additional
 conversion of recycled material is assumed in these cases. In refinery operations recycle of
 heavier cuts is often included for additional cracking of heavier cuts. Gary et al. [10] show
 an example of a hydrocracker yield structure with 35 vol % of the initial feed (coker gas oil
 + FCC decant oil) distilled and yielded as gas oil, which can potentially be recycled to the
 hydrocracker or a second stage hydrocracker. Recycle in such cases will be dictated by the
 yield structure and the yield targets for product cuts that are attainable within the reactor
 system configuration.

73. Comment: In Figure B-1, recommend including equipment labels in parenthesis to make it
 easier to follow. E.g., stabilization reactor (RX405).

 Response: We did not include the equipment type, e.g., “stabilization reactor”, on the
 diagram. Only the equipment number, e.g., RX405, is included in the diagram to avoid it
 becoming too crowded. The table immediately following the diagram provides the
 corresponding information about the type of equipment, and this is now mentioned in the
 Figure B-1 caption.

74. Comment: Is two years of catalyst lifetime supported by experimental data for extended
 time on stream testing at pilot scale? If not, this should be stated. Likewise, what is the
 assumed lifetime of the pre-treat catalyst section? Recommend a sensitivity case for catalyst
 lifetime and replacement costs/LCA impacts, beyond just liquid hourly space velocity.

 Response: The two-year catalyst lifetime is an assumption for the long term and not proven
 yet through long-term testing. This has been noted along with a sensitivity analysis
 presented in Section 3.4.4.14. The pretreatment catalyst cost is assumed to be lumped with
 the main reactor catalyst (Section 3.2.13) and thus also assumed to have the same lifetime.
 Depending on experimental results and how elaborate the pretreatment configuration is, one
 may consider parallel and redundant pretreatment train(s) to allow achieving the same
 catalyst changeout or maintenance cycles.

 301

75. Comment: Recommend including necessary hydrotreater pilot plant physical parameters
 and test campaign durations in the appendix. This would include minimum reactor tube
 diameters and L/D ratios, liquid flow rate, gas flux, etc. Extended test campaigns (e.g.,
 1000-2000 hours) should also be included to ensure modeling results and conclusions are
 based on sound pilot plant de-risking data.

 Response: We included data from bench-scale experiments available in the literature along
 with relevant references. We hope more information will be publicly available in the future,
 especially from pilot-scale testing.

76. Comment: There may be a need to stabilize bulk oil supplies before processing CFP oil
 material in other units. In addition to stability, there may be other concerns like metals
 contamination, molecular weight, etc.

 Response: Content has been added to the conclusion section suggesting the potential need
 for pre-stabilization if there are variations in CFP oil supplies beyond acceptable limits.

77. Comment: Language in a previous draft seemed to advocate repurposing existing
 hydrotreating units for rapid scale-up of stand-alone upgrading without doing the
 appropriate work to identify and mitigate risks at smaller scales. Is this what the authors
 intended?

 Response: This was not the intent, and the need for experiments enabling incremental scale-
 up has been added to the conclusion of the final version. Additional feedback, beyond this
 review process, suggested that the capital investment barrier for stand-alone units can be
 significant in the near term, and especially difficult to justify without significant stable
 supplies of CFP oil. Thus, some of the near-term industrial scale-up efforts may pursue
 incremental introduction of CFP oils in coprocessing setups when CFP oils become
 available in larger volumes.

78. Comment: Feed filters should be included, e.g., in Figure 13.

 Response: We did not add filters to Figure 13 and other relevant figures with flow diagrams
 but understand that those are important to include in the feed system. We have added “(feed
 filters not shown)” to the caption of Figure 13 to note the need for feed filters in the process.

79. Comment: In the case of the use of existing equipment, metaling up may be required. Was
 that captured in costing? One needs to be careful of metallurgy choices. One of the big
 suitability criteria for CFP oil coprocessing is potential acidity and corrosion management.

 Response: The cost of metaling up parts of the process was not explicitly included in the
 cost estimates when existing equipment was reused for coprocessing in the conceptual
 design cases. As mentioned in the report, additional retrofit costs for each equipment were
 estimated by subtracting ‘the cost of equipment with the new capacities necessary for
 coprocessing’ from ‘the cost estimate of the corresponding existing equipment’. The base

 302

 case for existing equipment costs are stated in Appendix K (under “Repurposed Case ID” in
 the tables). A zero cost was assumed when this difference was negative for any specific
 equipment. A separate CFP oil feed system is proposed to address some of the potential
 corrosion issues, to allow the improvement of chemical properties of the CFP oil before it
 enters the regular petroleum hydroprocessing equipment. Additional detailed assessments of
 metallurgy are necessary for specific implementations.

80. Comment: The 90% onstream factor for hydroprocessing (in the previous draft) was very
 low relative to conventional refinery operations. Onstream factors should be at least 95%
 and even then, that is not even 3rd quartile.

 Response: We changed the hydroprocessing onstream factor to 95% in the final report per
 reviewer feedback.

81. Comment: It is good that relatively recent, real-world cost estimates were used for the
 hydroprocessing section. Try to include some estimates after the recent super-inflation as a
 sensitivity case.

 Response: As mentioned in Appendix B, KBR cost estimates were based on the first quarter
 of 2024. The basis for this report is 2020 U.S. dollars. Known indices, cited in this report,
 were used to convert to 2020 dollars. The reader may use the same indices to estimate costs
 for a different cost year.

82. Comment: In the previous draft we assumed that beyond a 20% increase in operational
 burden, the equipment would have to be replaced or reconfigured to handle CFP oil
 coprocessing or repurposing. A reviewer asked what constraints, such as exotherms etc.
 dictated this decision. Note that each unit may already be bottlenecked by some particular
 design choice or history of modifications.

 Response: In the final version we did not include the threshold criteria of 20% mentioned in
 the comment. We simply subtracted the cost of equipment with the new capacities necessary
 for coprocessing from the cost estimate of the corresponding existing equipment to estimate
 additional costs of retrofits. This was done for simplicity and consistency of our methods,
 and also understanding that some of the potential 20% design leeway in a unit may already
 be used up by the refinery during different modifications in the unit’s lifetime. For
 simplicity, the scaling variable for each unit (see Table B-2 column “Scaling Unit”) was
 used as the resizing criterion/basis for each unit, understanding that other constraints beyond
 our chosen scaling variable may become the basis for resizing a specific unit.

83. Comment: With the choice of surrogate model compounds, it will be good to understand
 how well they represent the actual chemistry.

 Response: Details of surrogate compounds for each feed stream are included in Appendix C
 (for CFP oils) and Appendix D (for SRD, LCO, VGO). Appendix L provides surrogate
 compounds in the product streams. As noted in the report, we did not focus on matching the

 303

 properties of product streams with fuel specifications, instead we used a consistent H:C ratio
 of ~1.8 in the overall liquid product after hydroprocessing for consistency across all cases
 presented, especially to capture hydrogen consumption and exotherms. Note that we did not
 have sufficient data for developing predictive reaction/reactor models for this report;
 development of such reaction/reactor models in the future can further enhance the value of
 similar process modeling exercises. While we defer the capture of reactor chemistry to the
 future development of predictive reactor models, our response to Comment 20 shows that
 we captured the energy and mass flow impacts adequately in our models.

84. Comment: Why are biomass feedstock and CFP presented here. The main focus is on
 hydroprocessing, and one might just state that CFP oil is an input for hydroprocessing and
 has a purchase price.

 Response: We wanted to present a picture for the entire supply chain including biomass
 feedstock and CFP. Although we leveraged previous work for presenting those sections, we
 felt it would be important since the biggest potential for improving maturity, increasing
 supply volumes, and reducing costs for this pathway currently lie in those front-end steps.
 Other reviewers also recommended that we present a detailed basis for our CFP
 assumptions.

85. Comment: It should be mentioned that hydrogen consumption is higher for CFP oils
 relative to HDS/HDN in part because (a) oxygen levels are higher than typical S/N levels
 and (b) because of the chemistry (ring saturation is needed to completely remove phenolic
 oxygen).

 Response: Content has been added in the final version to address the reviewer’s comment.

86. Comment: The hydroprocessing base capacity of 75,000 barrels per day is for relatively
 large units and may not represent many smaller refineries or units. This large capacity may
 also imply large amounts of CFP oil are needed to coprocess significant percentages in the

 Response: We acknowledge that the 75K BPD capacity is large. We maintained that for
 consistency in our process models. However, we have added separate sensitivity cases
 (Section 3.4.4.12) to explore smaller capacity units as well.

87. Comment: Clarify the volume of catalyst assumed. For the coprocessing case this will
 likely limit the quantity of CFP oil one can include because of heat management and reduce

 use product recycle for thermal management).

 Response: The assumptions regarding WHSV are included in Section (3.2.7) of the final
 version. Cases showing required curtailment of fresh feed for heat management are also
 included. A constraints-mitigation-based approach was not the format in which results are

 304

 presented in this report, although we have all the information necessary for such an analysis;
 this may be the subject of future publications if resources permit.

88. Comment: Recycle of liquid with the front-end feed should be included as an option, in
 addition to inter-bed recycle.

 Response: This has been included in the final report (Section 3.4.4.2).

89. Comment: Why was the configuration shown in Section 3.2.4 (for hydrotreating followed
 by hydrocracking) adopted?

 Response: As noted in the final version, this was adopted primarily because we had some
 bench-scale experimental data reflecting this 1-stage configuration with two catalysts for
 hydrotreating and hydrocracking in series. The hydrocracker is intended to crack and
 convert heavy molecules to fuel blendstocks. This is consistent with refinery operations
 where fuel-range molecules are recovered through fractionation and heavier cuts are further
 processed in heavy feed conversion units like fluid catalytic cracking and hydrocracking.

90. Comment: In Section 3.2.5 of the final version, it is stated that in many petroleum refining
 configurations processing heavier feedstocks such as LCO/VGO, the recycled liquid is a
 heavier cut after distillation that requires further cracking. This was not included in this
 report. It may be important to understand those recycle effects but understood that currently
 available data does not allow accurate modeling.

 Response: We will look into this in the future if relevant experimental data becomes
 available.

91. Comment: It was stated in the draft version that when liquid recycle is used for temperature
 control (L-TC) the hydrogen concentration at the reactor outlet can be specified. This may
 not be realistic, and it should be determined by process operations.

 Response: This has been clarified in Section 3.2.5 of the final version, stating that this was
 only a modeling assumption, and that actual operations may need modifications based on
 reactor and process needs. Note that the model also captures equipment capacity changes
 (and associated costs) when an assumption of hydrogen concentration for L-TC cases is
 applied. Our criteria for maintaining a hydrogen flow of approximately four times the
 chemical hydrogen demand for the L-TC cases is mentioned in Section 3.4.4.2.

92. Comment: As with the quality of CFP oil, the petroleum streams used as co-feed will also
 likely impact stability; one should use caution if extrapolating the initial bench-scale
 experimental results too far.

 Response: This is a valuable comment, and we are noting it in this Appendix for future
 reference. Current data sets used as the basis for our models are limited to SRD and

 305

 LCO/VGO blends. This aspect will be further explored as new experimental data becomes
 available with different biomass derived intermediates and base fossil feedstocks.

93. Comment: There are a lot of nuances during catalyst stability testing. Encourage a breakout
 section or a section in the Appendix to explain more details of experiments, such as time
 onstream for the tests, conditions run, and % CFP oil tested.

 Response: Details are provided in Appendix A of the final report.

94. Comment: While hydrodeoxygenation may inhibit HDS and HDN, the reverse may also be
 true.

 Response: Per the reviewer’s comment, a note has been added that there may be inhibitory
 impacts and competition among HDS, HDN, and hydrodeoxygenation, and those effects

95. Comment: When mentioning the removal of CO2 with H2S using an amine system, point

 Response: Content has been added to the final version per this comment.

96. Comment: With respect to controlling CO in the gas stream, some vendors promote
 conversion of CO to methane to avoid permanent catalyst deactivation.

 Response: The reviewer’s comment is noted here.

97. Comment: CO2 may not be totally consumed/removed in the amine system, based on
 current coprocessing experience. This depends on the quantity of CO2 produced and the
 amine capacity.

 Response: A statement has been added in the final version of this report, citing that this may
 happen depending on the biogenic feedstock, such as for HEFA coprocessing. Inert CO2
 remaining in the fuel gas system (downstream of amine treatment) is considered low risk but
 remains an important consideration for refinery operations as it will reduce the heating value
 of the fuel gas supply.

98. Comment: Put more emphasis on the CFP oil feeding system.

 Response: We included a CFP oil feed system with a relatively simple design that includes
 catalyst within enhanced piping (Section 3.2.13). It is also noted that more extensive designs
 may be necessary depending on the CFP oil and its chemical behavior. On the other
 extreme, separate and elaborate catalytic stabilization reactors are necessary for non-
 catalytic pyrolysis oils.

 306

99. Comment: Running CFP oil reduces capacity. Is this opportunity cost accounted for in the
 analysis?

 Response: The opportunity cost does not appear in the MFSP calculations. However, the
 downside of reduced capacity is mentioned in discussion content, noting that refineries may
 have slim margins and reducing processing capacity can impact the bottom-lines of these
 units. There will be optimization opportunities that can be assessed for specific situations;
 those cannot be effectively analyzed in a generic setup such as the one presented in this
 report. Crude and CFP oil prices, associated incentives, processing costs, and other key
 inputs for the specific location will need to be considered for such optimization analysis.

100. Comment: Are different crude costs or margin environments considered?

 Response: We did not consider different crude costs and margin environments. The MFSP
 is calculated to provide estimated price points for the different feed options, with our
 underlying assumptions. The modeling framework can be adapted for such analysis, but that
 was not considered for this report. Only the impact of changing unit costs of
 hydroprocessing feeds on the MFSP is discussed in this report (Section 3.4.4.15).

101. Comment: The cost basis for petroleum feeds, CFP oil, and hydrogen should be included.

 Response: Those are now included in the final version. It is noted that these major unit costs
 may be prorated by the reader to estimate results with their own assumptions.

102. Comment: Are the cost of feed system modifications for CFP oil included in the MFSP?

 Response: The costs are included based on the assumptions described in Section 3.2.13.

103. Comment: Pertaining to the text in the report about small refining margins and how they
 can be affected by the introduction of CFP oil, it will be helpful to see the details of
 additional hydrogen consumption when CFP oil is introduced at different levels. It would
 also be helpful to include more details about how the yield vectors change as a function of
 percent CFP oil cofed.

 Response: Appendix K of the final version includes both hydrogen consumption and cost
 information for all the cases we studied. As discussed in the report, we do not have
 sufficient experimental data at this point to understand yield vectors. Key assumptions for
 yields in our models are included in the report (a) 90% carbon efficiency for CFP oil to
 hydrocarbons, (b) ~1.8 H:C ratio in the total hydrocarbon fuel product, (c) complete
 deoxygenation of CFP oil.

104. Comment: The MFSP charts at 75k BPD show that capital is an insignificant factor vs.
 feedstock, which is typical for commodity processes, and especially true for fuel production.
 Does this imply that the operating configuration matters less than the coprocessed amount,
 the source of the CFP oil, and other factors? That is what the figures imply.

 307

 Response: That was also our conclusion from this analysis, but sensitivity analysis for lower
 processing capacities (Section 3.4.4.12) show a larger impact of capital costs on the MFSP,
 as is expected.

105. Comment: One might investigate CI minimization strategies like purge minimization (i.e.
 allowing H2 purity to drop w/associated catalyst lifetime impact), purge gas reprocessing, or
 additional recycle gas purification.

 Response: Some of these impacts are presented in sensitivity cases in the final version. A
 PSA is included within our system analysis boundary to capture and recycle hydrogen; this
 is accounted in our CI calculations.

106. Comment: Since a constant capacity of 75k BPD is maintained in most of the cases even as
 the WHSV was changed, was reactor volume adjusted to maintain constant processing
 capacity?

 Response: That is correct, the reactor volume was adjusted. In this report we estimated
 capital costs by scaling using the total flow into the reactor. For cost estimation purposes,
 this scaling may be done using other process metrics, such as changes in the catalyst
 volume. In the future we can report results based on constraints of existing equipment, such
 as using the criteria for maintaining the same reactor volume after curtailing capacity when
 CFP oil is introduced.

107. Comment: It would be helpful to clarify the impact that WHSV has on the unit. Is the
 assumption a lower throughput at lower WHSV? Are there any yield implications or H2
 uptake impacts assumed for a change in WHSV.

 Response: As explained in the final version of the report, we assumed chemical hydrogen
 consumption as the basis for adjusting the WHSV. This approach was adopted because we
 currently do not have the necessary experimental data to develop predictive reactor models.

108. Comment: One would expect the temperature control problem to be more pronounced when
 running high CFP oil content feeds. Considering catalyst deactivation due to hot spots/H2
 starvation effects in such situations may be out of scope for this report.

 Response: That is correct, we did not consider detailed behavior that may be seen inside the
 reactor. The process model explores macro aspects. However, one can notice that our model
 assumptions automatically increase the hydrogen flow based on reaction needs and those
 larger flows will likely alleviate some of these concerns. These aspects need to be evaluated
 in detail for any specific installation.

109. Comment: The results in the report (earlier draft version) will be more pronounced by
 running higher proportions of CFP oil in the feed or CFP oils with high hydrogen
 consumption.

 308

 Response: That is correct, and this is illustrated by the results in the final report.

110. Comment: Provide more explanation related to hydrogen consumption, e.g., high purge rate
 leads to higher H2 losses for the low H2 supply purity case.

 Response: This is included in the final report (Section 3.4.4.5).

111. Comment: Suggest a deeper explanation of the effects of process changes throughout the
 report, rather than just reporting cost changes. This will lead to more interesting process
 operational choices to model.

 Response: We did not have sufficient time for those write-ups when we prepared the initial
 draft. Explanations are available in the final version.

112. Comment: Discuss the stability challenges of raw fast pyrolysis liquid which leads to the
 need for producing stable pyrolysis derived intermediates.

 Response: We included a review article on the hydrotreatment of fast pyrolysis liquid
 intermediates in Section 1.1. We did not discuss those challenges in this report, but the
 interested reader may refer to the review article and other related references.

113. Comment: When discussing FCC vs. hydroprocessing mention the distillate/gasoline ratio.
 Heavier fuels from hydroprocessing may be desirable for supplying heavy-duty vehicles
 because of the electrification of light-duty vehicles.

 Response: We mentioned that FCC products are typically dominated by the gasoline-range
    fuel (Section 1.1.2).

114. Comment: For hydroprocessing catalyst, another adjustment (handle) could be reaction
 temperature and therefore consequently reduced lifetime.

 Response: We discuss catalyst lifetime, and the process impacts of temperature separately
 as sensitivity cases. Those impacts can be approached concurrently if the relation is

115. Comment: The stabilization reactor is the key for ensuring the two years lifetime of the
 main hydrotreater. It will handle all coke precursors and inorganic contaminants in the
 biofeed. This unit operation has not been well defined for CFP oils. It will be important to
 provide the assumption of this unit. What are the operating cost inputs for the stabilization
 reactor? This may need to be regenerated or refilled frequently.

 Response: Our assumptions regarding the CFP oil feed system are included in Section
 3.2.13 of the final report. A minimal design is proposed in that section, but the design may
 need to be more elaborate based on long-term testing. If necessary, a redundant train will

 309

 need to be added so it can be isolated for regeneration without interrupting the entire
 hydroprocessing operation. Ultimately the design should be determined after experimental
 evaluation with specific feedstocks and catalysts in use.

116. Comment: For co-hydroprocessing, changes in gas yields and gas composition are
 important because recirculation of gas is common in hydrotreaters. Changes in gas make
 would also impact downstream gas stripper design.

 Response: Although a predictive model was not used for yields, the significant differences
 in gas yields from petroleum feedstocks vs. CFP oils are generally captured in the process
 models. The modeling framework factors in changes in gas yields when estimating
 equipment and operating costs for each case.

117. Comment: What impact did coprocessing have on conversion? From a refining
 perspective, maintaining product quality upon co-feeding CFP oils will be an important
 criteria.

 Response: Quality aspects for diesel and jet fuels are discussed in Section 3.1. Impacts of
 using CFP oil as hydroprocessing feed are stated, such as the necessity to saturate aromatics
 to improve the cetane number of the fuel. Impacts of CFP oil coprocessing on product
 quality and cuts will need to be factored into refinery economics after understanding the
 chemistry. Maintaining identical product slates will be difficult when high proportions of
 CFP oils are introduced, but those impacts can be modeled before implementation if
 experimental data is available. Decision-making will ultimately be guided by overall
 economics based on policy and demand tradeoffs for specific locations (and many other
 criteria guiding the overall company business).

118. Comment: Regarding hydroprocessing design types (1-stage vs. 2-stage included in the
 report), one can make the distinction by reactor types instead: fixed bed (trickle or in some
 cases vapor) or slurry reactors. Almost all industrial reactors are packed with multiple layers
 of catalyst, with staging included for functions such as treating or cracking, heat
 management, hydrogen addition etc.

 Response: We included the 1-stage and 2-stage distinction in Section 3.2.1, highlighting
 primarily that there is no intermediate processing, such as separation of certain products, as
 the reactants pass through different beds and reactor functions (treating and cracking);
 intermediate injection of hydrogen and liquid are included within our interpretation of a 1-
 stage configuration. All hydroprocessing reactors assessed in this project are assumed to be
 distillate-range units. Based on operating conditions, all reactors are fixed, trickle bed
 designs. There are no naphtha hydrotreaters (vapor phase) or resid units (slurry reactors)
 considered in this analysis.

119. Comment: In a previous draft, it was stated that single-stage systems may have limited
 performance. An absolute statement regarding limited performance cannot be made because
 this depends on the feed stream.

 310

 Response: We removed the “limited performance” from our description of the single-stage
 system but maintained that they have less operational flexibility compared to two-stage
 systems.

120. Comment: The yield structures in Table A-2 (Appendix A) suggest hydrocracking severity
 was very low (low conversion). Many refineries use hydrocracking to reduce residue and
 maximize more valuable streams. Implicit in Section 3.2.1, the two-stage hydrocracking
 option is portrayed to offer flexibility; however, this flexibility will be limited by refinery-
 wide optimization (same for design/operational choices for each hydrotreater). Returning to
 the low severity experimental hydrocracking conditions with CFP oil (Table A-2), was it
 done deliberately to avoid cracking CFP oils too far? This local choice of conditions may
 not represent overall optimization strategies adopted by refiners.

 Response: This is a valuable comment. We hope that additional experimental data will
 allow the development of models that predict reaction behavior when CFP oils are
 introduced. These predictive reactor models can then be used by refinery optimization
 models that can determine the optimal choices for refinery operations when CFP oil is
 introduced. The proportions of CFP oils will greatly impact the optimal choices.

121. Comment: Regarding the use of the total condensed liquid product (prior to fractionation)
 as a potential means for cooling the reactor, it may be more favorable if the post-
 fractionation heavy product is recirculated instead of the total liquid product.

 Response: As mentioned in the text, the recycle of heavy products is a common industrial
 implementation. However, we made the simplifying assumption of total liquid product
 recycle, assuming it is nearly inert after the first-pass of hydroprocessing. This allows easy
 interpretation of the results for assessing the impact of CFP oil processing without
 superimposing additional hydrocracking reactions that are typical with the recycle of the
 fractionated heavy products.

122. Comment: If there are many hydrocarbons going with the gaseous feed stream to the amine
 scrubber per the model outputs there may be accumulation of hydrocarbons with increased
 surface tension and foaming leading to operational problems.

 Response: Stream(s) 12 in Appendix L show the process model compositions of the amine
 scrubber feed for four bookend cases (100% CFP oil and 100% petroleum feed). These
 compositions (with the caveat that these are modeled compositions) may be assessed from a
 high level for potential operational problems such as foaming. Molecular weight ranges of
 hydrocarbon species after HP Flash will likely be similar with and without CFP oil feed,
 although their proportions will usually change (may increase if there are higher amounts of
 smaller fragmented molecules formed during the removal of oxygen bonds in CFP oil)
 relative to petroleum feed hydroprocessing. The other big difference will be the presence of
 large proportions of CO2 after CFP oil hydroprocessing. As mentioned in the text, CO
 formation has been reported to be low in the CFP oil bench-scale hydroprocessing

 311

 experiments; the potential presence of CO may be another difference between gases formed
 from CFP oils and petroleum feed hydroprocessing.

123. Comment: Makeup hydrogen is added to cover chemical hydrogen consumption and purge
 losses. For coprocessing, if there’s much CO/CO2 and light hydrocarbons then the purge can
 become significant, requiring more makeup hydrogen to manage purity

 Response: The increased purge required during CFP oil hydroprocessing has been captured
 in the process models which include design specifications to maintain specified purities of
 hydrogen.

124. Comment: The process models assume an H:C ratio of ~1.8 in the total liquid fuel products.
 Hydrocrackers are usually operated with a measure of conversion of the heavy feed, and not
 H:C ratio of the products. The H:C choices for hydrotreater and hydrocracker products
 should have been different, since they operate at different severities and have different
 chemical functions.

 Response: In practice these assumptions will need to change, especially when specific
 situations and associated strategies have been identified. The combinations of cases with the
 different hydroprocessing feed options considered in this report require different and
 complicated assumptions after careful analysis, especially in tandem with any experimental
 data available. Since we attempted to generalize and present high-level impacts in this report
 it was important for us to have the simplified consistent assumptions included for this
 analysis. We do present some sensitivity cases to show the impacts of changing some of our
 assumptions. Our simplified assumption reflects hydrogen and other resources required for
 the ultimate conversion of all feedstocks (including CFP oil and petroleum) to usable
 products; in practice this may be achieved after multi-pass recycle of the heavier
 fractionated molecules in the petroleum feed to a hydrocracker. Repeating from the report,
 the recycle of unconverted hydrocarbons after fractionation of the hydrocracker products
 was not included since we did not have predictive reactor models that could be adapted to
 reflect reaction behavior after the introduction of CFP oil feed. We believe our simplified
 assumptions allow easy interpretation of the impacts of the introduction of CFP oils with
 existing petroleum feed, delineated from the complexities of individual hydroprocessing
 operations.

125. Comment: Process safety would require options for rapid depressurization of the
 hydrocracker and significantly more thermometry to avoid runaway exothermic reactions.
 Does scaling up for higher pressure ratings account for these requirements?

 Response: The cost prediction correlations with ranges included in Appendix B capture a
 broad range of operating conditions, configurations, and operations. Some of these
 requirements should be captured by those methods and correlations.

 312

126. Comment: Hydroprocessing unit operators prefer to change temperature rather than space
 velocity when making operational adjustments. Adjustments in space velocities result in de-
 rating the unit, and there isn’t usually spare “space velocity”.

 Response: We considered space velocity adjustments since de-rating is an important option
 when oxygenated feeds are introduced, resulting in higher hydrogen demand and associated
 higher exotherm. We appreciate the reviewer’s comment and understand that de-rating
 decisions will be taken at a higher level, but unit operators will likely continue to use
 temperature adjustments (within allowable ranges) as the primary means to control reactor
 performance after baseline operational parameters have been determined.

127. Comment: The simplifying assumption for calculating costs by splitting the costs evenly
 between the hydrotreating and hydrocracking reactors (mentioned in Section 3.2.7) will be
 different in practice. These are two different reactors with different catalysts and hydrogen
 consumptions.

 Response: We agree and have noted this comment here. This is a simplifying assumption
 and can be revisited when we have more data to provide additional granularity for the
 reactor system. Our preliminary bench-scale experiments for hydrotreating + hydrocracking
 (that we used as a potential basis) were configured with two sequential catalyst beds with no
 intermediate operations between the catalyst beds.

128. Comment: Regarding the minimal implementation of the modified feed system in Section
 3.2.13, miscibility and thermal stability evaluations will be important for scale-up.
 Depending on the service, fouling may already be a concern with an existing feed, and it
 will likely be exacerbated by addition of CFP oil.

 Response: We agree that the system shown in Section 3.2.13 is minimal. More elaborate
 systems, including stabilization reactors may be necessary for more reactive feedstocks,
 especially if attempting to hydroprocess non-catalytic fast pyrolysis liquids. As the reviewer
 pointed out, interactions with existing feed will also need to be assessed.

129. Comment: Most of the metrics are presented on an energy basis. Refiners use volume basis
 (not energy) for road fuels, aviation fuel, etc. It would be useful to have volumetric
 information presented side-by-side.

 Response: We used energy basis to avoid potential errors that often get introduced with
 liquid density calculations. To provide an idea of the metrics corresponding to average
 volume bases for different fuels we provided the MFSP metrics in dollars per GGE, GJE,
 and GDE in the results tables (Appendix K). We agree with the reviewer’s comment that
 refiners make choices based on market conditions, but we did not pick specific market
 drivers for determining price differentials among different fuels; we instead used an energy
 basis to determine the MFSP of the entire fuel pool.

 313

130. Comment: As with the MFSP sensitivity with the on-stream factor, the MFSP changes with
 WHSV does not capture the economic impact on the refinery. Changes in WHSV are
 associated with changes in throughput and capacity curtailment which affects the bottom
 line of the refinery.

 Response: We added text to point out that the MFSP metric does not fully capture the
 economic impact for the refiner in these sensitivity cases.

131. Comment: Regarding the stated basis for hydrogen consumption and WHSV corrections:
 chemical hydrogen consumption in the 100% SRD hydrotreater model, 615 scf/barrel of
 fresh SRD feed with a corresponding WHSV of 2 h-1 was used as the basis. The 615
 scf/barrel is relatively low and a WHSV of 2 h-1 is on the high side.

 Response: We understand that these values can change with the petroleum feedstock
 chosen, as shown in Table 28. We picked the relatively mild hydrotreating case with SRD as
 the basis, although we could have picked a higher hydrogen consumption with lower WHSV
 based on another petroleum feedstock, as shown in Table 28. We chose the SRD case
 because it was already modeled within this same framework and we could ensure
 consistency when we analyzed other feedstock combinations.
